the agenda includes the discussion of the commission statement on the devastating bushfires in australia and other extreme weather events due to climate change (2020/2504 ((rsp)).
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, i think i’ll wait a second until it calms down a little bit.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, despite the dramatic and, i would even say, tragic events we are going to discuss in a minute, this is the first time we have seen each other after the christmas break, and therefore i would like to wish all of you, the citizens you represent and the whole european union, all the best for the new year and happiness in 2020. we know that it will be a very challenging year and therefore i very much appreciate that you have already put as the first item on the plenary agenda this urgent debate on the devastating bush fires in australia. as you know, my commissioner colleagues are now coming to strasbourg from luxembourg, where they have been pledging the oath to their office at the european court of justice, and therefore it would be my pleasure and my honour to represent the european commission in the afternoon’s plenary debates with you. as you very well know – and as australia has confirmed – these fires which we are going to debate are unprecedented. they have devastated local communities, they have led to the tragic loss of homes and they have taken the lives of people and, possibly, of over a billion animals, many of which are completely unique in the world. beyond the immediate destruction of forest, land, lives, animals and ecosystems, these wildfires jeopardise health and livelihoods in other places as well. canberra experienced peak air pollution levels that were 20 times above the hazardous threshold, to give just one example. first and foremost, we send our support and solidarity to australia. the european union stands ready to help the australian people. yesterday, my colleague commissioner lenarčič reached out and spoke to the australian minister littleproud and he reiterated the union’s readiness to assist australia in this moment of crisis. he also informed the minister of the possibility for, in particular, firefighters to be deployed at short notice under the union civil protection mechanism. the minister appreciated the support and readiness of the union to help, but he reiterated that at this stage they don’t require additional assistance. the science is loud and clear. climate change will increase the frequency of extreme weather events and these events will continue to affect us, and with more and more consequences and higher and higher intensity. do we need more proof that the prevention of climate change and environmental destruction, adaptation and disaster risk prevention, are absolutely necessary, and that we need to mitigate the effects of climate change together in europe, in australia and in the rest of the world? we have discussed many times in recent months the importance of eu leadership on global climate action. the effective implementation of the paris agreement and increasing global ambition is a key priority under the european green deal. the european union will use all the tools at our disposal to tackle the global climate challenge. our bilateral engagement with partner countries is crucial, in particular with the g20 economies that are responsible for three-quarters of global emissions. we value our long-standing partnership with australia and are working to strengthen it through the establishment of the dialogue on climate action under the framework agreement. the commission thanks the parliament for tabling this urgent debate and for seeing the climate urgency in these tragic events and especially in these circumstances, where we need to highlight the need to take global action. this is yet another proof of europe’s ambition to take leadership against climate change, and on this the commission will always remain on your side.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission is not prepared to accept this. i would like to thank you very much for your report, commissioner šefčovič. let me make two comments on this. firstly, with the commission's relaunch, the launch of the green deal and the follow-up to the world climate conference in madrid, the eu must add a climate and environment dimension to its foreign policy more clearly than before. we need more than just summits and conferences, we need constant promotion of the paris climate targets and the content of the green deal. the future eu climate diplomacy must be strengthened in terms of personnel and, above all, institutional and competence, so that we can quickly bring on board partners like australia, which have been difficult to reach so far. and secondly, we have to face up to the fact that the way in which fires are being fought has been called into question. australia's firefighting system is one of the best in the world, yet it has been overwhelmed by the novel fire dynamics. we must face the challenge of living with the fire or leaving certain areas uninhabited in the future. in 2018, forest fires in greece killed 100 people. australian vegetation is found in many parts of the world, and here in europe, another hot summer may be coming. therefore, ladies and gentlemen of the council and the commission, please take up the new challenge of preventing and adapting to firestorms and strengthening european climate diplomacy.
on behalf of the id group. the commission is not prepared to accept this. what bothers me most about the debate is the sensational satisfaction of left-wing climate neurotics with the terrible fires in australia, under the motto: here is finally the proof of man-made climate change. shame on you, and shame on you loud and clear. without wishing to sound cynical, devastating fires have repeatedly erupted from the fires that are part of the continent's ecosystem: 1851, 1939, 1983, 1974, 1975, for example, were such years. what about the drought? in fact, there is a trend towards higher annual precipitation, as you can see here in this descriptive statistic of the last 120 years, even though 2019 was particularly low in precipitation. the causes of the fires? among other things, it was not only arson but also the crooked policies of green environmentalists that prevented government programs to deliberately burn dead wood. this has massively increased the so-called burden of fire. even small bushfires can become disastrous. these facts do not make the suffering of the victims any more bearable, nor do they make the drama in the wild.
i have to say, madam president, the fires in australia are a nightmare, and you sit there and ask yourself all the time: what can we do, what can we do as europeans? it is clear that our solidarity is with australia, but solidarity is not enough. we need to look very closely at how our economic relations with australia are fuelling the climate. because now even prime minister morrison is recognizing that there is a connection between the climate crisis and the fires. example 1: the eu is planning to conclude a free trade agreement with australia later this year. this agreement will lead to increased emissions, as the commission's own official analysis shows. for example, because the increasing meat production is taking up more and more grazing land, and because we are shipping meat and other products all over the world. and that's going to continue to heat up the climate. example number two: we all know we need to get off coal as quickly as possible, and yet australia is planning to build one of the largest coal mines in the world with adani, together with european companies like siemens. australia is burning, and siemens is putting the adani deal ahead of the paris climate accord. both examples show that we finally need a change in the eu's trade and foreign economic policy.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. the european union has a long history of being a major player in the international criminal justice system. but organized crime has long since fully recognized the process of european integration as such. any act which is not prevented by the lack of police cooperation, the lack of harmonization in the european area, gives the enemies of europe the opportunity to polemicise against one of the most precious freedoms, namely the free movement of persons in europe. that is why we pro-europeans are called upon to be more courageous and to finally move forward decisively in police cooperation and justice. we have to get out of the national silos that we have. we need a european fbi against organised crime, and we need finally better cooperation between our security services. but above all we must protect one thing, which is the free movement of people within europe and the free movement of people, because this is one of the most precious freedoms on which europe is built.

in writing. free movement is considered by many to be one of the eu's great achievements. but it comes at a price. according to the 2017 european union serious and organised crime threat assessment report, some 5000 organised crime groups operate within the eu a 50% increase compared to 2013! the european monitoring centre for drugs and drug addiction also reports that the eu is experiencing the worst drug crisis since the eu was founded. and the attacks in paris, berlin and nice also show how terrorists exploit the freedom of movement. drugs, crime and terrorism are undermining civil societies. add to this the growing financial power of organized crime. the annual profits of organised crime in the eu area are already 110 billion euros! this too is changing societies for the worse. the situation in central american countries is a blueprint for what is also happening in some countries in south-eastern europe: the takeover of state institutions by financially powerful criminal cartels. when schengen was introduced, the eu promised that freedom of movement would not come at the expense of security. the opposite is true, organized crime and terrorism are growing unchecked! the danish authorities have also reintroduced border controls last week, as a result of the continuing security situation. the schengen system has failed. you should admit that!

on behalf of the id group. the commission is not prepared to accept this. the gender pay gap is also a modern myth, and the less you ask about it, the easier it is to get upset about it. there's a unadjusted and a adjusted pay gap. if we take the adjusted figures, the difference is between 2 and 7% for the younger people, those in their 20s and 30s, and even vice versa to the detriment of men. looking at the gender pay gap, we have to say that the cause lies in the politics of the political left, which still fails to value child-rearing and motherhood economically and to take them into account in the calculation of pensions, for example. we are seeing conservative governments like hungary, but also poland, whose family minister will be speaking here soon, go the way of taking into account the services that mothers provide when they care for children, so that the gender pay gap is decreasing, while precisely where women are seen as economic animals and family services are not paid, it is increasing, as in many western european countries. in this respect, it is a new ideological attack by the commission. it is necessary to protect family work and motherhood in order to promote women, not to have such phantom debates.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the period of validity of the directive to the period of 1 january 1999. the commission is not everyone needs a home. housing is a human right. to deny a person a home is to deprive him of his dignity and of this human right, of his participation in society and of his future. so if the european union wants to act on its own fundamental rights, it must finally intervene and set itself the clear goal of ending homelessness by 2030! ending homelessness is a matter of political will. finland shows us this with its housing first strategy - in my eyes, a paradigm shift in how to deal with homeless people, how to see them. homelessness is never just self-blame. hundreds of thousands of people in germany are homeless. in finland, this exclusion is almost non-existent. in order to meet the 2030 agenda for sustainable development and to put pressure on member states, the commission needs to require all member states to have a national strategy to prevent and address homelessness. we need an eu framework that gives recommendations on preventive and reactive measures and a corresponding monitoring of the objectives through the european semester.
the commission has already done so. i'm so grateful to you that after i announced that we would be putting homelessness on the agenda, we're finally talking about this important issue. i've been on the road for the last three years. i have looked after the homeless in my home in the ruhr area and since i have been a member of this parliament for the last six months i have also looked after the homeless in brussels and strasbourg. and i can tell you what i experienced there is horrible. i can invite each of you to take a tour with me under the bridges of strasbourg. i can assure you that there is no romance under the bridges of strasbourg. there is misery and want. we are witnessing a humanitarian catastrophe in europe, which is now affecting three million people. homelessness is the plague of the 21st century, and it's not just that we have no remedy, we ignore this huge problem. i've seen people just walk over the homeless. they're not even noticed anymore. we have no solution, not even close. we must finally address this important, real problem.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. from 2006 to 2015, the year of merkel's illegal border opening, about a quarter of a million people were homeless in germany. since then, the number of cases has increased rapidly. it is estimated that soon there will be more than a million homeless people in germany. official statistics on this do not exist. it is also tragic that many children, women, young people and even whole families are forced to live on the streets. in my home city of munich, the same picture is emerging: in 2015, there were about 5,000 homeless people, today there are 10,000. the reasons: lack of affordable housing, the shrinking stock of social housing and - we cannot ignore this - the impact on the housing market of uncontrolled illegal immigration. munich is being built as if there were no tomorrow. and for whom? for people who can afford to pay 10,000 euros per square metre or more, and for illegal immigrants and their family members. the low-income worker is left behind, lives on the streets or has to move into the community. i therefore call on the commission to do its job and close the external borders before there is social unrest or worse!
i have to say, madam president, mr president, i would like to begin by wishing you every success in your presidency. you have put forward important priorities, including the priority 'a europe that connects'. i think it's important that we also understand in this context that there are always accusations against politicians that they say one thing and do another. i think we also have to look at how we connect what we do with what we say. i think we will and we can exchange different views on asylum policy here in the coming months, but i think we should have this important exchange on the basis of being honest with each other. i am concerned that we see here, again and again, on the one hand - i have seen this myself in croatia - that there is so much evidence of pushbacks, so much evidence of human rights violations at the external borders, but on the other hand we see that it is not said: "that is true, we will clarify it", but that it is said: "it is not happening". i believe that if you want a europe that protects, then we must have a europe that also protects human rights at the external borders, and i hope that you will be more successful in that too.
the commission has already done so. it is now some 28 years since croatia was recognised as an independent state by most eu countries, and now you are sitting here presiding. i would like to congratulate you on this. i must admit, though, i was a little disappointed in your program. you talk about turkey being a candidate country, you want to admit kosovo to the eu. i don't think your founding president and party founder franjo tuđman would have supported such a policy, i can't imagine that the majority of people in croatia support such a policy. i can only warn you that you are not here, as a first-class european, to feel that you have to pursue a policy which is contrary to the interests of your people. look at how many people are leaving croatia, how young people are leaving your country. and if you take franjo tuđman and his book 'croatia and the question of nationality in the present day', you will find better answers to the challenges facing europe than what the commission and this parliament are doing. the european union is currently the largest global economy with the lowest growth performance, it has the lowest investment power, but it wants to spend three trillion euros on climate change, which is not necessarily a technology of the future, even if the majority of people here think otherwise. the solution to the question of europe is more likely to be found in tuđman than in brussels. i was hoping that croatia would bring the spirit of the 1990s here, so that we could be partners in a regionalisation and a return to common sense. the great states, the supranational entities, are of no use to us. you, the children of yugoslavia, know this better than most of us here.
on behalf of the id group. – mr president, dear colleagues, especially those who are here for the last time, the eu-uk withdrawal agreement is a salutary example of belated political realism. the resolution debated today accepts this verdict. three points, however, deserve mention. first, the agreement states that in future, the parties may decide no longer to accept national identity cards without a biometric identification chip. such a rule is not unlawful, but there seems no obvious legal, economic or security reason for it. data protection and privacy were once subjects of fierce political debates. sadly in our rights-obsessed political climate today, such basic rights seem forgotten. second, the agreement freezes eu citizens’ rights as they are being interpreted on brexit day, while assigning future interpretation to the court of justice of the european union (cjeu). the cjeu is, in effect, asked to develop two parallel bodies of case—law: one for citizens’ rights within the eu27 and another for the uk based on pre-brexit eu law. how realistic, i ask, is this? finally, clause 18 of the resolution bemoans the uk government’s intention to end free movement of persons next year. the motion claims that all four freedoms are interlinked and that any comprehensive eu-uk trade deal should be conditional on the uk agreeing to generous free movement of persons rules. this demand is against the uk’s and the eu’s own interests. free trade agreements – indeed, most eu trade agreements – generally say nothing about free movement of persons. the eu in 2018 had a very large trade surplus with the uk. it’s therefore in the eu’s interests, just as britain’s, to negotiate a comprehensive trade deal. if this house is serious about citizens’ rights, then eu and uk citizens have a right that their economic interests be taken seriously by this house.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, i wish to thank everyone in the commission who contributed to this proposal for a just transition fund (jtf), in particular commissioner ferreira and mr timmermans. i do sense the underlying spirit of a social just transition. people take centre stage and not companies, also a clear ‘no’ for subsidies for fossil fuels and nuclear power is just what the future needs. this proposal for a jtf is a good starting point to help particular regions to cope with the transition. however, what i’m missing in the proposal is a clear commitment to european level climate targets. the people and the economy need a clear date when the last european coal plant will get off the grid. we need commitments by every single region to individual coal phase-out plans. this must be a binding condition in order to receive support from the jtf, because funding a transition without the actual happening of a transition is pointless. and of course we have to talk about the money: eur 7.5 billion, seven years, 108 coal regions. this roughly comes down to 10 million per region, per year. is that enough to make the transition just for everyone? and in your calculation you then add co-financing from other cohesion funds. but this can be very dangerous because we don’t want to shift money from one region in need to another one in need. you then multiply the jtf with potential private investments, but mr timmermans please: we both know that there is not 100 billion in that just transition mechanism, but 7.5 billion. when street magicians try to trick their audience in such a way, we call that a sleight of hand. i’m sure the commission doesn’t want to be seen a street magicians. so please come clear and let us fight together for a better consistent funding from own eu resources, so that coal workers are not at the mercy of mff negotiations every seven years. we can support this proposal, though, if it stands for a clear and just transition with concrete phase-out plans for all coal regions.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. one of the greatest challenges is to think together about environmental, economic and social policy. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy is a very important one. the commission proposal for a just transition fund is right in principle. the poorest and most marginalised people in particular are facing the negative impacts of climate change. our job must be to protect these people and to provide them with social security. the proposed financial support, for example for retraining jobseekers, is welcome but will be far from sufficient. in order to tackle social exclusion comprehensively, we must also take account of people who are not active in the labour market. the goal of the sustainable development agenda is to end poverty in all its dimensions by 2030, not to make it worse. the path to a climate-neutral economy must be a joint, inclusive process that eliminates discrimination of any kind to the greatest extent possible. it is therefore essential that member states involve civil society and the social partners in the preparation, implementation and evaluation of their regional plans. it is also essential that we create social security mechanisms. it is the only way to prevent poverty and to help people in their transition. in this context, the european commission urgently needs to propose a framework directive on a minimum income.

in writing. the report on the eu's common foreign and security policy states that the eu's external action would have a direct impact on the well-being of eu citizens, as well as providing security and stability. but if we look at the situation in the mediterranean, we get a different impression. the so-called 'sea rescuers' who are helping criminal gangs to smuggle fish into the sea are not stopped. the eu commission often even pressures member states to allow boats to land. but this does not lead to greater security and stability in europe. on the contrary, it is causing more and more people to embark on the perilous journey across the mediterranean. and it leads to more and more illegal mass migration, which poses unsolvable challenges to our nation-states and especially our communities. the same is true of the safe harbor initiative, which aims to oblige municipalities to take in illegal immigrants. but such initiatives are the wrong incentive. they send the wrong signal and make sure that more people are in need, not less.
the next item is the joint debate on the report by mr mcallister, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, on the annual report on the implementation of the common foreign and security policy (2019/2136 (ini)) (a9-0054/2019) and by mr danjean, on behalf of the committee on foreign affairs, on the annual report on the implementation of the common security and defence policy (2019/2135 (ini)) (a9-0052/2019).
the commission has already made a number of proposals. the situation in the middle east is making it clear to us once again that more arms do not make a region safer, and what we need above all is mediation, disarmament and prevention. but unfortunately we find little of this in mr danjean's report on the csdp. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. and with european arms export policy, which is partly responsible for the build-up, everything can allegedly remain as it is. instead, the report repeatedly calls for military spending to be increased to 2% of gdp. i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the situation in the middle east, but i would like to say that we should not be concerned about the situation in the middle east, because the situation in the middle east is very serious. we therefore acknowledge mr danjean's attempts at mediation, but we will vote against the report if our substantive amendments are not adopted tomorrow.
i have to say, madam president, pesco, military mobility, armored roads, combat troops of all kinds and the european defence fund are just some of the buzzwords. the eu is now expected to cut back on social and regional aid projects in order to pour billions into the arms lobby. they are in breach of european law, because article 41 (2) prohibits spending from the eu budget on military action. yes, colleagues, it is true! the world political situation is fragile, and there are numerous conflicts. but to mention these in the report without mentioning the role of the eu and the member states is hypocritical. the eu's economic policy, trade policy and the growing number of arms exports from the member states are also fuelling these conflicts in the world. if you are serious about peace and democracy, then you must put an end to arms exports all over the world, and then you must put an end to this insane arms race. the european union must not be reduced to a military union, but must defend and extend disarmament treaties. that would be sustainable security policy, not what you're doing. it's more of a fire hazard.
i am sorry, but i have to say that i am not in favour of the amendments. the commission has already made a number of proposals. there is currently a fragile ceasefire in libya, and a serious political solution is indeed uncertain. in 2011, nato bombed libya and toppled the dictator gaddafi. the result was total chaos, a brutal civil war and, since then, essentially a proxy war. this nato war now has multiple actors in libya. in addition to regional powers, almost all the major powers are involved. they're all about oil and gas and geopolitics and spheres of influence and power. that is the bitter truth. but for the people there, war means death, suffering and misery. and what does the eu care about? what is mr borrell talking about? mostly about refugee protection! most eu countries are working with the sarraj government, which is also working with the so-called libyan coastguard militias to keep more and more refugees out of the european union. this has to stop! we must now stop arms exports to all parties to the conflict in libya, and all external powers must withdraw. this would be a contribution to a lasting peace in libya. the people there need peace, not further escalation. war and arms spirales will not bring peace - neither in libya nor anywhere else.
i have to say, madam president, i would also like to thank mr borrell for his performance and for his efforts to achieve peace in libya. i don't think anyone thinks that it's easy to secure peace in libya, but i do believe that we can do more, even as the war in libya rages on. since 2014, 32,638 people have drowned in the mediterranean, and i always wonder what we would be missing if those people were still alive. i've been to sea rescue in the mediterranean myself, and when you talk to the people who are being rescued there, you realize that in libya, they're just not treated like human beings. it is, of course, completely inhuman that we in europe should be helping to bring back directly into the war zone those people who are fleeing the war at the risk of their lives. i wonder why we as european states have sent more patrol boats to libyan militias than rescue boats to the mediterranean. i think we are humiliating ourselves by having an important discussion here, but not doing what is obviously possible and at least saving the people from drowning, fleeing from the war, from torture, from the detention camps in libya. i believe we could evacuate more people, we could also provide more rescue at sea in the mediterranean, and i actually believe that the european idea is that we should do just that.
i have to say, madam president, the deployment of the armed forces in libya is the next clever move of erdoğan in the blackmail game with the eu after the refugee deal. this will further consolidate its already very strong position as a gatekeeper of europe. and let 's face it . we are not the ones who are currently in control of the migration crisis, who are deciding the escape routes via the balkan route. this is erdoğan, this is turkey. it determines the extent of illegal mass immigration. erdoğan has to make a very slight threat of the next wave of refugees, and we'll be tracking it. but now a new dimension has been reached. now turkey wants to use libya to gain control of the african country from which most illegal migrants start their journey to europe. the consequence: erdoğan has europe in his stranglehold, we are even more vulnerable to blackmail. we must finally use our economic and military strength. we must continue to play this game as equals. the eu must therefore do everything in its power to end turkey's intervention in libya, to end the refugee deal and finally to take over border protection.

madam president, in 2000, the eu launched its lisbon agenda to make the eu ‘the most competitive and dynamic knowledge-based economy in the world by 2010’. as 2010 was approaching references to the agenda were expunged from eu websites and the plan was renamed the europe 2020 strategy. 2020 has arrived and the most competitive and dynamic knowledge economy, maybe as a moral champion of the world, but it also enjoys the highest consumer income and corporate taxes in the developed world, high unemployment and the lowest growth apart from japan. for the most integrated countries of the eurozone, economic data are even worse than for the more sensible non-euro eu members. the commission president now proposes a conference on the future of europe to make the eu more democratic. she mentions an economy for the people. yet also proposes more taxes and an eu green deal for eur one trillion plus, when eu influence on world climate is minimal. in the name of europe’s way of life, she’s advocating more non-european immigration to make europe less european by the day. eu democracy is democracy, neither for nor by the european peoples. sigmund freud spoke of ‘flucht in die krankheit’ or ‘escape into illness’ to describe a neurotic avoidance of an unsatisfactory reality. rather than face the struggle to regain health, the neurotic patient is secretly desiring to remain ill. is there a better diagnosis of the eu today? faced with a menacing economic truth, the eu commission is reaching for a narcotic overdose of eu integration to take an extended holiday from reality.
madam president! the future of europe is not the same as the future of the european union. europe existed before the union - we should remember this - and it will probably continue to exist even if the union ceases to exist or changes into some other form of political cooperation between the european nations. but there is no doubt that this difference should be taken into account when thinking about the future of both the union and europe. this debate is necessary because, on the one hand, there are increasing voices from prominent thinkers, both conservative and left-wing, who are saying that european civilization is in a critical, even decadent, state. on the other hand, we feel that the european union also needs reform. we are in favour of an open, honest debate, which does not exclude those with a different opinion, for example those opposed to further integration, and which does not prejudge the direction of the proposed changes. we cannot divide the citizens of europe into good and bad europeans, as some of my colleagues do when they speak of a european republic. we also agree that citizens should be involved in the debate on the future of the european union, but we know that there is no single european public sphere, and that this should be done at national level. we also believe that citizens' discussions cannot replace parliamentary debates and should only be in the nature of public consultation. we also recall that the treaties can only be amended in accordance with article 48 of the treaty on european union.
i have to say, madam president, the commission has given the european parliament a leading role in this space for debate, which is designed to last two years. that's good! and with today's resolution, parliament is making a concrete proposal to the council, the commission and the european public on the approach, objectives, forms and methods of how, together with all citizens, european policy can be redefined to meet so many of the challenges of the future as well as everyday problems. after years of painful crisis experience and attempts to cope, we must decide together what kind of eu we want and need, what needs to be changed or reworked. i think it is very important and right that parliament's core approach is to put the sovereign, the citizen, at the centre of the conference from the outset and to link representative democracy and citizen participation in determining the future framework for eu policy in a very innovative way in a permanent dialogue process. yes, a conference on the future of the eu is first and foremost about giving citizens a voice in their wishes, expectations and demands for the eu. they should decide for themselves which issues need to be addressed in order to eliminate democratic deficits, develop new political strategies and test the suitability of policies at eu and national level. why are you afraid of citizens who want to create a solidarity between the european continent? we need transparency and the use of today's modern means of communication. that is why the conference will have to go beyond the convention.
madam president, how often do you speak of the future of europe by identifying it with the future of the european union, a union of monopolies, which some people once portrayed as eternal, as law-abiding? today we are talking about brexit, decentralisation and contradictions, while in your own papers you are worried about the anaemic capitalist recovery. you are waging a campaign of brainwashing, provocative embellishment of the european union, manipulation and anti-communism, with a high price, in an attempt to stop the growing mistrust and mistrust of the peoples and the youth of europe. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european parliament is given the opportunity to express its views on the commission's proposals. contrary to what the gue group, which is a signatory to the resolution, claims, the european union is not changing, nor, as the far right claims, making mistakes. it serves the exploitative system, it is opposed to the interests of the working people, it is a constant underminer of the defence of borders and sovereign rights. the peoples can shape their own future by disengaging from the european union, with the people in power, for the europe of socialism.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i am a practicing lawyer in germany, and of course the judges are appointed by the ministry of justice. separation of powers means that the judge is free to decide on the individual case. but it is not for the sake of god that the judges choose themselves who becomes a judge, what the rules of procedure of the courts are, etc. it is a polish peculiarity that the judges themselves appoint their colleagues, their presidents and their successors. and it is the old practice, now outdated, that is eroding the separation of powers. the division of powers means that the judge is bound by the law, but it does not mean that he appoints himself, promotes himself and even checks himself whether he follows the rules. that's the difference. i am therefore very much impressed by the demand made here in the name of the separation of powers to maintain this very break in the separation of powers by the old rule. and i hope the polish government holds out.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, we welcome the new methodology on enlargement. the clusters give a new dynamic for the accession talks, but the fact that the cluster on fundamentals has special importance is highly appreciated by us. without functioning democracy, without rule of law, and even without good governance, there is nothing to talk about it. unfortunately, democratic institutions are not safe once we see progress. rule of law and media pluralism have been declining since serbia started negotiations in 2014, yet we pretend as if nothing has happened and continue the negotiations and other chapters, but without the fundamentals we cannot progress in other fields either. with a new approach, we can move forward when we see progress. the more ambitious countries need to be included in as many eu policies as possible. with this, we could gain back credibility and work on real changes in every citizen’s life in the region. the southeast of europe needs to become part of our green deal if we want to see europe climate—neutral by 2050. however, we should keep our promises. nothing is worse than the disappointment which the council produced during the last summit in october. it urgently needs a green light for north macedonia and albania. abolishing the visa regime for kosovo is overdue, as they have fulfilled all the criteria required. a new methodology is only meaningful if we keep ourselves to it as well.
the commission has already done so. why did the british leave the eu? because they wanted sovereignty over their country, but also because the eu has been breaking its central promise for years, namely the promise to increase the prosperity of european citizens. that's not the case. in fact, youth unemployment is dramatic in many countries, we are technologically dependent in many areas, productivity is falling, and the rescue of the euro and the associated zero-interest policy have made many europeans poor. but you ignore all this, as if there were no brexit, as if there were no yellow vests in france. now you want to include some balkan countries too! all these countries are at the bottom of the transparency index, in other words, highly corrupt, and all of them will be the recipients of transfers that will be in the pocket of european citizens. the enlargement of the eu to include more countries at the present time is another nail in the coffin of its existence. once you have solved the economic problems of the eu, you should look further.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the period of validity of the directive to the period of 1 january 1999. the commission has already done so. automated decision-making systems will literally decide our lives, and even more so our children's lives. education and employment, housing and employment, purchases and news are affected. but it's our decision what influence they have, what data the machines make judgments on, and what they decide to do and what they don't. we want ai to really serve everyone, and we can now set the course for that - with a clear yes to ai for climate protection and sustainability, but also with a clear no if it discriminates against people. because we know that every data set collected in an unequal society has a discriminatory effect. for example, women are currently paid less and do more caring work. this fact is reflected in the data and, if not corrected, will continue to make women poorly paid or even worse paid in the future. we must take legal action against such injustices with all our resolve. in the european union we have the right to safe products and services. but that right is only worth something if we can enforce it. so our regulators need broad powers to test algorithmic systems for legality. europe now has a unique opportunity to create a legal framework based on our democratic values that could serve as a model for the world. let us face this great undertaking with courage.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. code is law that is, what is written in machine code is in many cases as good as legislation, and therefore: bad code is also like a bad law. and what that means is that we're no longer just dealing with situations where algorithms actually make decisions, but in many cases, algorithms actually make decisions, or decisions are based on information that algorithms have calculated. this situation should not be underestimated, because the number displayed is impressive - even for a person who might theoretically make the decision - but so far we are only protected or there are only certain guarantees of transparency for the decisions made by the machine. i think that needs to change. we need transparency in the future, even where algorithms influence and dictate decisions, because the fact that a human being makes a decision cannot be just fiction and theory, but must also be guaranteed in practice.

madam president, let’s be clear: vietnam is a communist country. it’s not a democracy, and a trade agreement won’t make it one overnight. but the agreement will boost economic development. it will lift people out of poverty and give them more rights and protection. the new labour code especially protects vulnerable groups. unions will be allowed and freedom of assembly will follow. increased transparency and international observation bear huge potential for better protection of human rights. we are already trading with vietnam. this is not about if we want trade or not. this is about improving the circumstances. so let’s send a strong signal of cooperation to the whole asean region, to reformist forces that believe in cooperation with the european union, and let’s not give room to actors that don’t want to combine trade and values, like china. let’s support the agreement and let’s support the people of vietnam in their development.
madam president, this parliament decided in november that the paris agreement should be anchored in all chapters of our trade agreements, and this parliament decided in december that all our trade agreements would have enforceable environmental and social standards. also, this parliament considers itself to be at the forefront of human rights, and yet the majority of this house is ready to vote ‘yes’ on the trade and investment deal with vietnam, in a situation where human rights are deteriorating in vietnam and the numbers of political prisoners has been on the rise since 2016; in a situation where civic and political rights are systematically being cracked down; and in a situation where the deal on the table still does not contain enforceable social and environmental standards – and not only the social democrats in this house always pretended that this was a red line. i understood that there is a majority in this parliament to make trade more sustainable and climate-proof, which is good. but dear colleagues, we really make a fool of ourselves if we then, at the first best opportunity, give in and let a trade deal pass that does not live up to the green deal. i urge you today: say no to this deal. let’s ask the commission to renegotiate and to align the vietnam deal with the green deal. and let’s send a strong signal to the vietnamese government to improve the devastating human rights situation before we sign the deal and not after.

madam president, this trade agreement between the eu and vietnam is proof that we can establish european standards in the world through trade agreements. a significant improvement in workers’ rights; a ban on forced labour; the approval of independent unions; an enforcement mechanism for the paris agreement, which we wouldn’t have without the agreement. what a great success. colleagues from the greens and the left are generally against trade agreements. they share this in common with the right-wing populists, by the way. the question you have to answer is now: how do you want to implement european standards worldwide? through speeches in this house? demonstrations? letters? the only realistic way to promote european influence globally in an age of shifting geopolitical distribution of power is through trade agreements. those who vote against it will have to explain how they want to realistically achieve our common goals while, at the same time, following an agenda which is isolating and disabling the european union as a trading partner.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the figures show that in 2018 the trade in goods between the european union and vietnam amounted to some eur 40 million. this means that vietnam is already an important trading partner and that our citizens use products made in vietnam every day. this agreement can improve trade and political relations with a strategic partner in a very important area such as south-east asia, by framing these relations in a system of certain and shared rules. vietnam has in recent years embarked on a path of significant reforms, especially in labour law, the progress of which has been certified by the world labour organisation. of course, this is only a starting point and we should demand concrete deeds and actions from the vietnamese authorities both in this respect and in respect of human rights and freedom of expression. in this sense, the trade agreement represents an important lever in the hands of the european union to stimulate and call on vietnam to respect the commitments solemnly made.
madam president, mr commissioner, this will be the first major trade agreement that this new parliament will ratify, and it will send several positive messages. firstly, to an ever more protectionist world, that the eu still believes in rule-based free trade. secondly, to the region, bringing us one a step closer to a regional asean (association of southeast asian nations) agreement. thirdly, to our citizens and businesses, that we are dedicated to expanding better conditions for business to thrive, grow and generate more jobs. and finally, it is proved that trade can positively influence other areas. the negotiations helped push vietnam towards important labour reforms. by having this framework in place, we will now have a way to constructively engage on topics ranging from human rights to the environment and, as with any agreement, implementation will be the key. and you can count on this parliament to do our job.
madam president, agreements between the eu and vietnam are undoubtedly important geopolitically, and vietnam has also made progress in reforming its labour laws – but it has, on the other hand, failed to amend its penal code to allow workers to enjoy those rights. oppression has become more severe even during the last stage of the finalisation of these agreements. parliament, if giving its consent tomorrow, takes a heavy responsibility of making its own demands happen in reality on sustainable development, human rights and participation of an independent civil society, and, as our own committee on foreign affairs demands, the commission and its vietnamese counterparts still must set up an independent monitoring mechanism on human rights and an independent complaints mechanism with the recourse to remedy. so this all is why our group, the greens/european free alliance, cannot yet support these agreements.
madam president! both the free trade agreement (fta) and the investment protection agreement (ipa) are indeed the most ambitious agreements of their kind ever concluded with a developing country. in negotiating them, the commission sought to achieve two main objectives: first, to provide union operators with the best possible conditions for access to the vietnamese market; and second, to provide a valuable second reference point (after the agreement with singapore) for other negotiations conducted by the union in the region. both objectives were fully achieved. the agreements go beyond existing wto commitments in many areas, such as services, procurement, non-tariff barriers and the protection of intellectual property rights, including geographical indications. in all these areas, vietnam has also agreed to take on new commitments that go far beyond what it has committed to in other agreements, including the comprehensive and progressive agreement for trans-pacific partnership. the agreements will remove almost all customs restrictions on mutual trade. the commission notes that the union's macro-financial assistance is limited to the minimum necessary to address the specific needs of the union's macro-financial assistance. the eu will be able to exert greater pressure on hanoi on human rights issues through these agreements. that is why i call for a vote to ratify these documents.
madam president, please. i see the extremes are converging in this house today. i suggest the opposition reconsider our priorities. our job is to create opportunities for european companies and our citizens to benefit from them. more trade means a chance for vietnamese citizens to develop and to achieve their freedom by following our standards. it is a mistake to think that the union should be constantly educating and training instead of approaching its partners with a little respect and seeking good relations. dialogue and verification of commitments will contribute to the development of human rights, labour and environmental protection in vietnam, not turning our backs. those who argue against the two agreements do not want european companies to have access to the public procurement market in hanoi or ho chi minh city. dear opponents, please don't wish harm to european companies and vietnamese. i ask the commission to help provide up-to-date, practical information to enable european smes to make the most of the two conventions.
madam president, mr hogan, the agreement between the european union and vietnam is of great strategic importance because of the impetus it gives to our trade relations with the asean region and because of vietnam's firm commitment to defending multilateralism and sustainable development when the world needs it most. an ambitious tariff reduction, enhanced cooperation and, above all, a binding sustainable development chapter defining a framework for labour relations with vietnam more in line with ilo principles and conventions have been negotiated. the european parliament, thanks to the pressure of my group and other groups, has obtained concrete measures and commitments from the vietnamese government on labour rights and respect for human rights, which are specified in work programmes. and i want to thank the efforts made by the vietnamese authorities. but there is still a lot to be done and we socialists are still concerned about the human rights situation, especially as regards fundamental freedoms. we will therefore be monitoring closely the implementation of the agreement and i ask you, commissioner, to take the appropriate steps to ensure that this agreement does not harm european farmers and in particular rice producers. but i think this agreement is an important instrument for moving forward and i will support it.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. trade is basically mutually beneficial. as a regular rapporteur for bangladesh and other countries, i know how developing countries look at vietnam, because for the first time an emerging country is being given the opportunity to link up and develop with the european economy through an individual free trade agreement. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. that is why i am asking for your consent. the free trade agreement is balanced, it reflects the different views here in the house on trade with other countries, and it makes all the emerging countries a european offer for further development so that there is prosperity at home and not so that these people have to seek their happiness elsewhere and in other countries. in this respect, it is a good, balanced agreement that casts a positive light on europe and trade. i ask your consent.

madam president, i would urge everyone to step back from the numbers. the objectives are the most important when deciding on the multiannual financial perspective. will we not strive to ensure a good standard of living for all inhabitants of all regions? the numbers seem to indicate that, because cohesion is being cut very sharply. will we tolerate some farmers being treated as second-class citizens? this appears to be the case, as the commission proposal does not include the harmonisation of direct payments. do we put national interests ahead of our common goals? and this question is linked to the debate on the level of the member states' contribution to the general budget. i agree that the european union has new challenges, but new challenges need to be financed with new money. because you can only withdraw funding from specific areas when there is a general agreement that it is no longer worthwhile to pursue them.
madam president, commissioner, i have to ask you to stop. the cuts proposed by the previous commission in the eu budget for the two main policies, cohesion by 10% and the cap by 15%, have just been rejected in a position adopted in portugal by the 17 countries of the group of friends of cohesion at the beginning of february. the budget must therefore be increased both by raising membership fees and by eliminating existing rebates, and perhaps also by creating new sources of own revenue. a compromise in the council is possible, but only if we move away from negotiations based on a net payers/net beneficiaries structure and also take into account the benefits of the individual eu countries from participating in the common market and capital flows. net payers generally benefit very much from a share of the common market. germany, for example, pays 27 billion euros in contributions a year, and the benefits of the single market are worth 210 billion a year. in addition, the countries of western europe have other benefits from enlargement. one is dividends and interest on capital. only by taking account of all these flows is it possible, in my view, to have a rational discussion about the shape of the future budget and a chance to reach an agreement.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, next week the special european council will be dealing with a very important subject: what resources will the european union have from 2021 to 2027 to meet the new challenges we face and give european citizens a more environmentally sustainable, fairer, safer, more modern society? parliament has asked for an adequate budget. so far, the response of governments has been inadequate. on the one hand we are giving europe new tasks and responsibilities which governments cannot tackle alone, but on the other hand we are keeping the purse strings tight. how to say: to do more but without resources. the perfect recipe for continuing to use the european project as the best scapegoat for the things that are wrong. this is not a budget, ladies and gentlemen, but the credibility of the union itself. we therefore ask the council presidency to take the message of this house to the heads of state or government. we expect a reversal of the trend immediately, no more playing with the future of the union, with the future of all of us.

in writing. 70 years ago, the then french foreign minister robert schuman made his declaration, which is celebrated as the beginning of the process of european integration and the birth of today's european union. in effect, the german and french coal and steel industries were removed from national control and placed under a common european authority. this was the end of peace in europe. what is not known today is that robert schuman, jean monnet and other pioneers of european integration also embraced the idea of a euro-africa. access to african resources would enable europe to maintain its position as a strong continent. when the european economic community (eec) was founded in 1957, france still had a vast colonial empire. the system of preferential customs duties and financial subsidies was a prerequisite for the french government to join the eec. france saw the euro-african free trade area as a means of maintaining its hegemony in and over west africa. it is interesting that, following the civilization mandate of the berlin conference (18841885) formally divided africa , both the schuman declaration and the eec defined the association of overseas african territories as an instrument for improving the development of africa. in several subsequent translations of the declaration, the relevant sentences of the declaration were deleted.

in writing. we see here on the big stage the three fragments that make up this eu: breach of contract, breach of law and breach of trust! recovery bonds are just a relabelling of eurobonds under the guise of the covid-19 crisis, with some limitation of liability; but she'll strike right away. germany must vote 27 percent of the 500 billion reconstruction fund, proud 135 billion. unlike eurobonds, where the member states are only called upon to pay the default of guarantees, recovery bonds require the member state to pay the interest and repay its share directly. the german public debt will therefore increase directly in the medium term. the germans are already badly beaten. no country has taken in more refugees and fed them with social benefits, no country pays more child benefit to eu countries, sometimes even regardless of whether the children actually exist there. and almost no country has lower assets; italians and frenchmen have significantly more than the average german. in germany, raising children alone is a ticket to poverty. we therefore say no to all measures which are going to the pockets of the german taxpayer.

mr president, i would first of all like to thank all those in europe who have shown their solidarity. i would like to inform you and my colleagues that i have been the victim of police violence by the belgian police. i consider this to be an act of discrimination with a racist tendency. yesterday, i was leaving the north station and i saw nine policemen harassing two young black men. i had my phone in my hand, and i took a picture of the scene with my phone, which is legal. the police came to me, they snatched my phone from my hands. four of the nine armed police officers pushed me violently against the wall, violently took my handbag, pushed me against the wall with my legs apart, and one of them wanted to feel me and search me. they treated me in a humiliating way. when i told the policeman that i was a member of the european parliament, he did not believe me, even though he had my two passports in his hand, my european parliament pass and my german passport. he then asked me for my belgian residence card. i gave it to him. i took my badge out of my bag and i had the badge around me. i filed a complaint today because we can't let this police violence go unpunished. this experience, at a time when the whole world has seen the dire consequences of police brutality, is traumatic for me, but here i am, despite spending all night here in parliament writing, and not having had the courage to leave before 6am. i was here yesterday, we can check it with our badges. we must therefore take concrete action for the many people who are not here and who have not been able to escape police violence.
madam president, as a black woman in europe, i have not only become aware of the racist reality at home, i have lived the racist reality of europe for the last 40 years. what is happening in the world today is the result of colonial structures of oppression that have been in place for hundreds of years. it is not about individuals, it is about systemic and institutionalized racism that targets blacks and all ethnic minorities. i don't want to hear about police training, i want there to be consequences for police violence. i want justice for all those who lost their lives, those who lost loved ones and all those who were traumatized by the police. it is not just the black people of europe, but all black people and their descendants, people of colour, including those who continue to drown in the mediterranean because of racist immigration policies. i want to be clear: all black lives matter, whether they are european citizens or not, black trans lives matter, black women lives matter, our lives matter. as a teacher, i have seen how the souls of children have been destroyed by latent racism or open racism, by this word, the word "hate", which enters the soul of the child and ultimately ends in failures. it is time to renew dialogue so that no one is left behind and, as martin luther king said, we must learn to live together as brothers or we will all die together as fools.
i have to say, madam president, structural racism in europe, that seems a little strange to me. we see illegal immigrants in european cities demonstrating for a permanent right to stay. it doesn't necessarily feel like structural racism. we see in dijon street battles between maghrebis and chechens, without the police intervening. this does not seem like systematic state repression either. in many german, belgian, dutch and french cities, the police no longer dare to enter certain immigrant neighbourhoods. again, this doesn't seem like structural racism. and despite all the claims that racism exists here, hundreds of thousands of africans still want to go to europe. but there is a very simple remedy, if you really believe that europe is ruled by structural racism: stop the global compact on migration for at least ten years, until you have ensured that this alleged structural racism is removed from the european institutions.
in writing. of course, all forms of racism are to be condemned. and of course, the death of george floyd is to be regretted. every life is worth protecting. but what is happening in the united states and has spread to europe misses the real problem. in the united states, more than 40 percent of all blacks are middle and upper class. you are not a victim of society. the days of the united states being a racist country are over, thankfully. blacks have the same opportunity as whites to rise to the top of society. condoleezza rice, colin powell and of course barack obama are just a few examples. that is why this resolution sends the wrong signal. the claim that racism is everywhere is not only false but also corrosive. it perpetuates an imagined disability and relieves people of their duty to build their own lives and live in their own way. the left, supported by the other socialist parties, is exploiting and exploiting floyd's death to further its own political ends. it defines black people as victims who serve as a willing electorate to them. at the same time, it is trying to prevent any debate on the harmful effects of mass immigration by increasingly undemocratic laws. this development is detrimental to germany and the eu.
in writing. george floyd's death was a preventable injustice. he deserved a fair trial, as is the custom in a state governed by the rule of law. but his death does not justify violence or the destruction of statues. it is not acceptable to rewrite history or to attribute to western society a collective guilt for historical events such as the colonial period. the same is true of the fact that a vocal minority wants to impose its ideology on a silent and indifferent majority. every decent democrat has to fight back! the claim that western society is a hotbed of structural racism is sheer nonsense. this is used by the left-wing extremists as a pretext to destroy our culture and to stifle our resistance to their anti-democratic aspirations. only to establish their utopia of a multicultural state without borders. anyone who does not support them is denounced as a racist. we must preserve our culture and the roots of our identity. antifa is a terrorist organisation whose aim is to destroy democracy, the rule of law and its values, such as freedom of expression. it must therefore be prohibited to protect society. and that's what the afd and the id stand for, unlike all the other groups.
madam president, they ask us why we are doing this; they ask us why we stand united and invest so much time in negotiating something that the british government has rejected and ignored again and again. the answer is: we are doing it for our common european future. maybe not next year, maybe the year after that, maybe later, but this future will come. we are doing it for our common values and interests – values that don’t depend on the signature as a member of the eu: liberal democracy, climate policy, women’s and minority rights, human rights. these are values that we share with each other, regardless of membership of the eu. we are doing it for europeans on both sides of the channel, for those who share families, jobs, love stories, biographies. we are doing this for their rights. we are doing this for you, our british friends: british citizens who believe in the wisdom of the eu much more than you believe in the wisdom of your brexiteer government. because if they go low, we aim high, and we aim high with this resolution. we won’t ever see britain as a third country and we will never see british citizens as citizens of a third country. to paraphrase a famous brit: a brexit with a deal is the worst form of future for great britain, except for all other forms of brexit. this resolution is our last attempt to prevent the worst. the ball is in your court, mr johnson.
mr president, a good trade agreement is in the interest of both parties. but what do we see? the united kingdom has backed down. they will renegotiate the aspects already agreed. i think that really undermines the political declaration which was also signed by mr johnson. so the question is: does the british government want to make a deal? british business wants it. the confederation of british industry, the umbrella body for british industry, knows full well that our single market is essential. they realise that in the event of a no deal, there will be many losers on both sides of the channel. and the already shrinking british economy cannot possibly have that. i want an agreement with fair fisheries agreements, access for our fishermen to the whole of the north sea, good agreements on the joint management of fish stocks. and if there are no fisheries agreements, there will be no trade agreement!
madam president, vice-president šefčovič, the low expectations for the european council are undermining the climate of optimism and confidence that our citizens and our economies need. in my country, greece, after losing 25% of gross domestic product during the economic crisis, we are now facing a deep recession if the european union does not act immediately. the european council must therefore seize this opportunity to send a clear message of confidence to the markets, to show that the european union is ready to guarantee the future of its generation. the recovery fund, in conjunction with the community budget, can support high quality primary production, renewable electricity coupled with jobs in the circular economy, the digitisation of the public sector and its linkage with small and medium-sized enterprises that are now struggling to reopen. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we must create jobs for our generation and for the next generation.
rapporteur. – madam president, i want to thank my colleagues for their contribution to this debate and for their continued support. allow me also to say a special thanks to my colleague, co—rapporteur christophe hansen, and all the shadow rapporteurs who worked on this file. and of course, not to be forgotten, my gratitude also goes to michel barnier and his team for their full engagement with this house. we wish you patience and a good dose of humour in the coming weeks ahead. as you have all clearly underlined, what we need with this deal are not short-term answers but a long-term vision, given the unprecedented challenges we are confronted with. if the uk government does not immediately and radically change its approach to these negotiations, there is no way to reach any sort of comprehensive partnership before the end of the transition period. to be honest, under the current circumstances, i believe we must also prepare ourselves for a no—deal scenario. last week’s data from the uk office for national statistics and the organisation for economic co—operation and development show clearly the economic impact of the coronavirus crisis. it is time to turn the narrative to make the pandemic the reason for success and not the excuse for failure. that would be a disaster for citizens on both sides of the channel. we need a deal, and we can have a deal that is good for all of us. a no-deal is neither rational nor responsible. the next weeks are the moment of truth in many regards. the time has come for responsibility and courage. citizens and the business community need certainty. they need predictability. the eu is ready to play its role and to fulfil their needs and their legitimate aspirations.
rapporteur. – madam president, thank you, and i say to colleagues: thank you for all the contributions in this very long debate today. i wish i could respond to each and every one of your remarks, but given the time restrictions, i want to come back to the essentials, namely the many remarks i heard on the ‘level playing field’. my father, who doesn’t speak english, always asks me: ‘when i hear you speaking about brexit on the radio, why do you always insist on this level playing field? what does that even mean?’ so i told him, because we are both big cycling fans: ‘imagine the tour de france, all cyclists competing with a common race bike, except for chris froome with his sky team riding with e-bikes. do you believe this would allow for a fair competition?’ the answer was of course, ‘no, this is not what we want’. we want fair competition and fair play is what every good sportsman should aim for in a competition. this parliament wants the closest possible cooperation with the united kingdom. this is in everyone’s best interest. we will remain constructive and will be ready to discuss the fair and balanced compromises that will hopefully emerge in the coming weeks here in brussels and in london. of course, it takes two to tango, as the commission president earlier said. and, you have to be aware, this parliament will be ready for this last dance.
