talking about the agreement which was approved, signed and ratified by this parliament and by the uk parliament, and once there was a clear statement by michael gove that there will not be any extension of the transitional period, i did the same. and what i heard from our rapporteurs, ms piri and mr hansen, but also from mr mcallister was that we need to accelerate our work and to make sure that the withdrawal agreement is properly implemented. i’m very glad to report that the discussion of the joint committee was in a very positive atmosphere and that we could state that there is good progress in the area of the financial provisions, on the protocol on gibraltar, on the protocol on the sovereign base areas of the uk in cyprus and also on citizens’ rights where i know how important this issue is for the european parliament and for our citizens and i believe we will be able to resolve our differences definitely before the end of the year. what is the most challenging issue is of course the application of the protocol on ireland and northern ireland, and here i was very clear and i made it very clear also to the uk delegations that what we need is some more operation, because we understand the aspiration, which has already presented to us. but for us, it’s very important to have very concrete responses on how the uk is going to implement the vat regime, excise and customs obligations, which are clearly stated in the withdrawal agreement. i’m glad that the response i got from michael gove was that we will work very hard over the summer, that we will have another specialised committee session on the irish protocol in july, and we will come back overall what i believe will be progress in early september. so there is a lot of work, but i believe that when it comes to the withdrawal agreement, both sides are realising that the proper implementation is the key to trust-building, to confidence-building which we need for the good future relationship between european union and the united kingdom. and how will this future relationship look? if you allow, madam president, i will pass the floor to michel barnier because he’s negotiating day and night with our uk colleagues and he will be able to give you the best picture.
head of the task force on relations with the united kingdom. madam president, good evening to each and every one of you and thank you to maroš for the good cooperation between us. i would like to say a few words in conclusion alongside him on the record of this very complex, extraordinary brexit negotiation. first of all, i would like to thank your parliament, madam president, the 17 committees and their rapporteurs. as david mcallister said, this is the first time, and i think it is the time, and so i would like to thank very warmly kati piri and christophe hansen for this rather complicated work of synthesis that they have done and congratulate them on it. we need the vigilance and determination of the european parliament in this period. this is the determination that your president, david sassoli, expressed on monday at the high-level conference with commission president ursula von der leyen, charles michel, the president of the european council, yourself and boris johnson. and then, i think it was nathalie loiseau who said, you will have the last word, once again, when we have found, i hope, an agreement with the united kingdom. as maroš sĕfčovič has just said, we have taken note of the negative position of the british government to extend, as we have had, as we always have the possibility, until 30 june, this transitional period which is in fact the negotiation period. and so that puts us in the middle of the aisle: four months of negotiations behind us, only four months of negotiations ahead of us, kati piri said 204 days earlier. so it's difficult. it is still possible, time is very short and i share the sense of urgency expressed by boris johnson and that is why we have decided, with my counterpart, david frost and his negotiating team, which is, like ours, very professional, to intensify these negotiations in july, august and september, to have more concentrated, more targeted discussions, with the objective and obligation for us, but you know that this is my rule, to have the same transparency with your parliament, your coordination group, your committees and also with the 27 member states on whose behalf we are negotiating. what are the next steps? one thing is clear, as the three presidents of the three institutions recalled when they met boris johnson on monday, what is important to us, even before the process, is progress on the substance. it is up to the uk to choose what it wants and whether it wants a deal. you have said very well, minister brnjac, just now at the beginning of this session, that it is the choice of the united kingdom whether or not it wants an agreement because the conditions for an agreement are already known. we agreed them with boris johnson and every comma, every word was bitterly negotiated in the political declaration to commit ourselves to an ambitious and lasting partnership to which your parliament is also committed. not only an unprecedented trade partnership, zero tariffs, zero quotas on all goods - a first in the union's trade history - but also an agreement on fisheries, mr bellamy said, and an agreement on a level playing field, which will cover state aid, taxation issues, but also social rights and environmental rights. as christophe hansen said just now, an agreement on economic and social fair play, and this agreement will also have to cover energy, transport, security and defence issues, and on the basis of this unprecedented offer, we also want the conditions for access to our market to be fair. we have adopted this political declaration and these principles in our mandate and in our draft agreement published on 18 march, and this is not open to negotiation. after four years of negotiations, we can see, ladies and gentlemen, what the british government's strategy is. first, he refused to discuss any important issues, defence, security, development. i interpreted this as the idea of concentrating the whole negotiation on economic issues only and thus on defending its economic interests. and then, in this strategy, the uk is trying to negotiate, in some areas, a status very close to membership of the single market, of quasi-membership of the single market of the customs union and schengen without having any of the constraints or disciplines. this is what we call cherry picking, on rules of origin, on mutual recognition, on financial services, on professional qualifications, on data flows or on electricity exchanges. we will not accept cherry picking. the second part of the uk strategy is to reject all clear and strong commitments to a level playing field so that it can keep the freedom to deviate from the current eu rules and to make regulatory competition, some even say that this is the reason for brexit and we see this strategy on the data. i know the very firm position of parliament on this subject, on financial services, on state aid, where the united kingdom has so far given no indication of the future national framework for state aid, i see it also on food standards and, in this context, the united kingdom even wished to reopen the question of geographical indications, which are clearly protected in the withdrawal agreement mentioned by maroš sĕfčovič. all this is not compatible with the basis of a lasting, ambitious agreement with a great country which will remain our friend, ally and partner in any event. and yet i remain convinced, ladies and gentlemen, that an agreement is possible in the long term, as danuta hübner has said. we have wanted this from the beginning and we want to give this negotiation every chance, as this intensified process shows. but, ladies and gentlemen, as you have indicated in your resolution, we will not agree at any price, as mr lange has just said. we will never sacrifice - to use the words of our president ursula von der leyen - the long-term economic and political interests of consumers and businesses in the union for the benefit of the united kingdom. at the same time, we want to see tangible progress on the implementation of the withdrawal agreement referred to by maroš sĕfčovič, both the guarantee of citizens' rights and, madam president, the issue so important for peace in ireland, namely the proper application of the protocol. finally, while our priority remains and will remain to reach an agreement, we also have the responsibility, as mr arimont and mr schiderwan said earlier, to prepare collectively for all possible scenarios at the end of the year, in case of agreement or in case of disagreement. in any case, we must prepare ourselves and we will prepare ourselves. i wanted to thank you for your confidence in the european commission's negotiating team, which is, remember, your negotiating team. in the next few weeks, which will be crucial, we will have to demonstrate certain qualities. mrs. piri asked me to be humorous; i recognise that i have some room for improvement, but it is important at my age to keep some room for improvement. the calm to which mr. danielsson invited me, our patience; the calm and patience on my part are inexhaustible. our firmness, which is clear and sharp on the basis of the principles to defend our consumers and our companies. and finally, our unity, which parliament will give further proof of today, and for which i thank you.
thank you very much, and i hope your reserves do not run out, mr barnier, so good luck with the negotiations.

partnership negotiations had to go hand in hand with progress in the implementation of the withdrawal agreement. this means that we will have to remain vigilant as regards, first and foremost, the preservation of citizens’ rights, be it during the transition period or under whatever future relationship with the united kingdom. but our vigilance will have to be no less thorough when considering the intended economic partnership, where strong, safeguarded and robust governance will be called for to protect the integrity of the eu single market and the competitive position of eu firms. we will therefore count on your cooperation in this respect as well. in negotiation with the uk on our future relationship and partnership, our common efforts and commonality of purpose will provide the support required by our negotiator and help ensure an outcome to the benefit of the union and its citizens. coming back to the mff and recovery, the president of the european council wants to pave the in writing. the eu's demands in the trade negotiations with the uk are harsh and unreasonable. the refusal to grant london a trade agreement modelled on the eu-canada agreement and the eu's demand that the ecj should have the final say on the interpretation of an eu-uk trade agreement are inappropriate. in particular, this demand that the uk, as a sovereign state once again, submit not to an independent arbitration tribunal but to the highest court of its contracting partner, the eu, is contrary both to the practice of international law and to the practice of the eu itself. in almost all other eu trade agreements, the settlement of disputes is carried out by independent arbitration tribunals. in addition, the court's claim to power and impartiality is controversial even within the eu. the federal constitutional court held that while the ecj may monitor compliance with the eu treaties, the national constitutional courts have the final say on whether the eu is still moving within the limits set by the treaties. so far, london has refused to respond to the eu's exaggerated demands. if trade negotiations fail, not only british but also european exporters, consumers and workers will be harmed. after the brexit vote and the uk's exit, it is time for the eu to finally end its punitive expedition against the former eu member.
in writing. the next multiannual financial framework of the european union and the recovery fund must offer a solution acceptable to all member states; the proposal must leave no one behind. the budget must therefore be fair and balanced, and the money must be distributed fairly. the budget should be flexible in the use of funds, as there is no one-size-fits-all crisis management solution. the money is different in different countries, the priorities are different in the south of europe, different in the north and different again in central europe. the recovery of the cee member states should be based on a strengthened cohesion policy which has already proven itself; it has created added value in europe and its contractual objectives are as relevant today as they were before the crisis: to close the development gap and thereby strengthen the union's competitiveness. hungary has implemented significant reforms in recent years and is successfully catching up thanks to its efforts. however, the proposed allocation criteria for the resources of the recovery facility are based on pre-pandemic statistics and therefore do not reflect the real economic impact of the crisis. and they punish countries with good growth and low unemployment. the current proposal has a moral problem: rich countries would get more money than poorer ones. this cannot continue to be the case; member states with a disciplined economic policy and successful defence cannot be penalized by depriving resources. poorer countries must not be put at a disadvantage compared to richer member states.
in writing. – at this week’s european council, participants should be laying the foundations for a mutually—acceptable agreement on the upcoming multiannual financial framework and the recovery package as presented by the commission on 27 may. it is essential that the recovery package achieve its target to help relaunch the european economy. meanwhile, the long—term goals of the union, especially those focused on cohesion between its territories, must be upheld. we face an unprecedented crisis in the amplitude and depth of the peril it entails for the european economic system. that should be recognised in deeds, not just in words. to deal with the crisis satisfactorily, the criteria of the recent past hardly apply. unless some way is found for effective european action that really addresses, head on, the dangers of the ongoing recession where they are being experienced, the negative social and economic impacts will multiply. with all its flaws, the plans presented by the von der leyen commission go a good way towards meeting this vital challenge. its thrust has to be endorsed. arguments about, among others, whether loans or grants should be deployed; how recovery projects should be selected and conditioned, etc. should be subordinated to this overall priority.
in writing. i hope that the negotiators will be able to intensify the negotiations now so that a 'no deal brexit' can be avoided in the end. comparable environmental and social standards are as urgently needed to ensure fair competition as agreements on fisheries policy or police and judicial cooperation.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, i have 90 seconds of speaking time today. that’s the same amount that the european council took to discuss the outcome of the last major exercise of citizen consultation. we talked to hundreds of thousands of europeans in over 1 600 citizens’ dialogues. when i read now the draft council mandate, i have to say that i am shocked the outcome of the two-year debate on the conference should simply be another discussion of the council behind closed doors. how do you explain to citizens that we are having a debate on the future of europe, but that you don't really want them to change anything, that we’re excluding the possibility of treaty change, of real change, before the debate has even started? i think that’s absurd. so we are in a situation where, for the second time in a decade, we are slipping into the worst economic crisis. millions of europeans are losing their jobs, particularly young people. many of them had just gotten out of the last crisis. the question now is: can we or do we want to afford losing another decade, or are we finally going to address the structural shortcomings of this union, and make sure that we get out of this crisis faster than the last one. i hear in the council that the conference has become a bit of a running joke, a running gag. i think this house has made it very clear that citizen participation is not a joke, that meaningful reform of the union is not a joke, and that coming out of this crisis stronger and more united is not a joke. so the council really needs to stop joking around now. let’s start working on the future of europe, i think it is about time.
i have to say, madam president, in strasbourg i spoke of sigmund freud and the neurotic escape into illness: illness frees from the toil of recovery and promises relief and a holiday from reality. prophetic words! the corona crisis is the latest of countless eu and euro crises: politicians are wrestling with their hands, as always, not knowing what to do. the only thing everyone knows is that we need more eu now! the stage was set for the conference on the future of europe: the eu must become effective, democratic and close to its citizens. the aim is not to hold citizens' votes, but to organise structured citizens' dialogues, orchestrated by the eu and pro-eu ngos. and in effect, this is called: patronage, redistribution and over-indebtedness. the result is not recovery but descent. because the problem with the eu is that it will eventually run out of german money. germany and the eu belong on the couch of joy.
i have to say, madam president, we have a parliament here wondering why not a single institution of the european union is taking the first step towards the future of europe. because we are not deciding on treaty changes at the moment, it is not a complex mandate, it is an agreement on a mandate to enter into negotiations, to shape this conference at all. that is why this resolution is designed to make a clear appeal and say: how can this be? for six months - january, february, march, april, may, june - it has been impossible to agree on a simple mandate. instead, you stay in the trenches, you've dug yourself in, the blockages persist, and you can't make the first move. if we fail to take this first step, and if the council fails to do so, then we are all caught up in this and we will not be able to stabilise europe. so we say again, we've heard it, it's for us to say, how come you can't make that first step? the appeal also to the german presidency of the council: if you can get this done, get this moving, we must start now!

mr president, mr borrell, dear colleagues, we have focused our efforts on the covid-19 pandemic as a health crisis in recent months. but in its shadow a human rights crisis is unfolding and this human rights crisis is making the health emergency even worse. if regimes attack and kidnap journalists who report on the pandemic, it doesn’t stop the virus, it just spreads disinformation and mistrust. if regimes use corona funds to buy weapons for their police to enforce lockdowns, it doesn’t stop the virus. it only spreads fear and death. of course, restrictions to individual freedoms are necessary to fight the pandemic, but each and every restriction needs to be proportionate. it needs to be clearly linked to the health emergency and above all, people have to be allowed to debate them and often they are not. so, to fight the covid-19 crisis globally, we need to tackle both the health emergency and the human rights crisis. we need to monitor the spread of the disease and the human rights violations. we need to investigate deaths and human rights violations. we need to protect our doctors and our human rights defenders. mr borrell, it’s not just about millions. team europe needs to become team human rights. and while i applaud your efforts in this regard, and you have spoken up strongly, i really think that we need to step up our actions on this one as well, looking at the world of today.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, it should be clear to us there will never be a silver bullet to solve all problems of disinformation. but hey, we are in politics, and we all know there is rarely a one-size-fits-all approach. but we should be clear about our starting point, and the starting point is to defend our democracy, not to damage it. that’s why our strategy should be threefold and should be holistic. first, fight geopolitical propaganda attacks from outside. second, educate and inform press and internet consumers. and third, protect personal opinion and free speech. the past weeks have demonstrated the pitfalls, but also the chances that we saw. there was a strong sense of direction from the european institutions, and i’m grateful to mr burrell for framing it right. it’s not about just fake news, it’s about a debate and a battle of narratives. on the other hand, we saw how we can end up in an anti-democratic trap. this is why criminalisation of alleged misinformation about covid, like in hungary, attempted prevention of true press information in poland, prosecution of pharmaceutical representatives in bulgaria – just because they wanted to warn about a lack of drugs. this is all unacceptable. we should not lose sight of our strategic principle. by confronting disinformation, we must defend democracy, not destroy it. and one more point, because many people represent it here. anonymity is an important good in the internet. anonymity protects women, minorities, opposition voices in the internet, and that’s why we should never abolish this there. one more point on education and then i’m done. we should invest more in education, in justice and value systems and not cut this by 20% as is planned now in the mff, and we should invest more as part of the recovery in civil society and education and not disregard it as is planned now. this is the way to go. this is the way to protect democracy.

i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. 35 years of schengen an incredible success story! and it is precisely the actions of the member states in the face of corona - with many border closures - that have shown, as if under a burning glass: schengen is not taken for granted, but it is urgently needed. open borders in europe are essential for the exchange of goods, services and medical aid, but there is much more to it. the daily crossing of a border, living and working or studying in two countries, cross-border leisure activities, daily interacting across borders: this is the building block for understanding, despite or precisely because of many differences. i therefore urge the commission and the council - and of course the member states - to strengthen and extend the schengen area and let us live europe together every day!
mr president, madam commissioner, dear ylva johansson, at the beginning of the covid crisis in mid-march, europe was fragmented. most member states have reintroduced border controls. so thousands of citizens, border workers, families have been stranded, separated. i have seen it as a resident of a border town: strasbourg. we must all learn from these unprecedented events. i propose that before this summer we organise a retex, as the military calls it, that is to say a review of experience to analyse what has happened, to see what could have been done better. we must provide operational answers. i am, like many of my colleagues, in favour of a more integrated mechanism for managing our borders. i therefore propose that we be prepared to deal with any new threat to free movement, in particular in the event of a resurgence of the epidemic. as president lópez aguilar has said, free movement is a key factor in the recovery of the economy and employment.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. it is a difficult time we are living in, and perhaps it is especially important in these difficult times to reassess how fortunate we are to be living in this europe at this time. imagine that you were born 100 years ago, that world war i had ended only two years earlier, that the spanish flu had killed 50 million people, that your childhood had been marked by the great depression, and that when you were 13 years old, the nazis came to power in germany. by the time we're 25, the nazis have led germany and the world to the brink. if you were my age, 32 years old, it would be nine years before the berlin wall was built and an iron curtain divided europe. i think that we should also translate this happiness of not being born 100 years ago into a responsibility, especially in times of crisis like this, especially with a virus like this, where we say: we realize what we have achieved, what achievements we have, and we want to find effective measures now to target the virus, not the european project, as unfortunately happened in this crisis.
mr president, commissioner, i would like to correct those of you who have said that national governments have been wrong in their reaction. no, ladies and gentlemen, the european commission is wrong. it is precisely the european commission that has failed on the issue of 'border surveillance'. with its completely inadequate, absolutely inexplicable behaviour, it has forced national governments to take the necessary measures and close internal borders one after the other. a large part of you never understood that in order to have open internal borders, there must be strictly guarded external borders. you can't protect both of those at the same time, you can't want hundreds of thousands of illegal immigrants to come here unmolested and have secure internal borders. this can't happen, this is utopia and you've seen it with the panic and the hysteria around this crisis. we cannot fail to mention the totally irresponsible behaviour of the governments of the netherlands or now the netherlands, of germany, which with their internal problems are keeping romania, bulgaria, croatia and malta out of the schengen area. this is hypocrisy and double standards and it must be stopped.
mr president, i would like to thank you. when we discuss schengen, we must not forget that it is not just about free movement within the eu and having holiday plans without the passport in your pocket. schengen has been and continues to be associated with the closure of the eu's external borders. it's now reached levels that are terrible to watch. we see it in ceuta and melilla, we see it on the greek border, we see it in the balkans. we are seeing a policy that puts the protection of external borders above the protection of people. but our open internal borders cannot be built on the crime, violations and brutality of the external borders. one of the worst examples today is unfortunately the presidency of croatia, where the partly eu-funded border police commit systematic illegal push-backs. i've been there, i've seen it. the commission has a responsibility to investigate this, but also to put an end to croatia's schengen membership. a country should not be rewarded - no country, croatia or any other - for violating human rights at the eu's external borders.
mr president, i was three when the berlin wall fell. i was 13 when schengen became an integral part of the amsterdam treaty. i was about to finish school when 10 new member states joined our european family. europe was always part of my life. i have experienced it as open, as free and as a promising continent and to be honest, i never expected to see border controls again. yet, understandably due to the nature of the pandemic, and the force with which it hit us, closing borders without coordination caused chaos at the beginning, and even long—forgotten hard rhetoric came up again. the good thing is, we experienced how closely connected we are already, not only across border regions, not only economically, and we saw that schengen, that our freedom, is vulnerable, that it is invaluable, and that we need to protect it. luckily, schengen is getting back on track. europe is getting back on track, with a lot of work ahead of us. so let us draw the obvious lessons learned in the past weeks. let’s prepare schengen better and make full use of it for europe’s recovery. let’s make europe come out stronger. on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, first of all, i want to express my solidarity with the people in the amazon region and especially in brazil suffering from the outbreak of covid—19 and losing family members and friends. in this situation, what can be more shocking than hearing a minister calling to get rid of environmental protection while people are distracted by covid—19? this is what brazilian environment minister, ricardo salles, was caught saying on video. deforestation in the amazon keeps increasing and this is a direct consequence of government decisions. control agencies dismantled, penalty payments for illegal logging stopped and, if this were not enough, the bolsonaro administration is supporting a law which would legalise land grabbing in the amazon. this law could be passed by congress at any moment. i support everyone on the ground fighting these destructive policies and call on my colleagues in the brazilian congress to stop this course of action, especially the law on land grabbing. but, of course, we in europe also have a role to play. supermarkets in the uk threatened to boycott agricultural products from brazil if the land grabbing law is passed. in the space of a week, 350 000 people in germany have signed a petition to ask german supermarkets to do the same and we urgently need deforestation—free supply chains and to end our dependency on the soil. let’s face it, i think the mercosur agreement is dead. resistance was big before, but now in this line that leads brazilian environmental policy, i don’t see any chance for that deal.
i have to say, madam president, the commission has already made a number of proposals. deep in the north of the brazilian amazon, there is the indigenous community of the ituna-itatá. their territory, the terra indígena ituna-itatá, is protected by law. theoretically, but in fact, more and more loggers, gold miners, cattle ranchers, soybean farmers are invading these protected areas, making the area the most damaged indigenous area in brazil in 2019. various laws already enacted or planned by the bolsonaro government to weaken environmental authorities, facilitate land grabbing and amnesty land theft show little sign of improvement. this is a social catastrophe, but it's also a huge ecological catastrophe, because with the amazon, one of the most important and richest in species is being destroyed, one of the most important combatants against climate change. but this is not just a brazilian issue. europe also has a responsibility, because 10% of the world's deforestation is caused by us and our consumption in the european union, for example, of soy, palm oil, meat and rubber. many companies are aware of the problem and are working to produce more sustainably, but those who continue to cut down forests are profiting handsomely. we must not just look at it, we must find answers. this week i presented my draft report on an eu legal framework to curb and reverse global deforestation. that is why i am calling for companies that want to bring risky products to the european market soon, and the financial institutions that support them, to pay much more attention to where their products come from and under what circumstances they were produced. that requires mandatory duties of care. we're making companies commit that their products have not contributed to the destruction of forests, the damage of ecosystems or the violation of human rights. the ituna-itatá community would also benefit. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. the ituna-itatá cannot rely on their government to provide them with legal certainty and to assist them in legal disputes to combat land grabbing and environmental destruction. this is the case for many people in countries with weak legal systems or where there is no political will to do so, such as in brazil. that is why they must have access to european courts. when european companies fail to meet their due diligence obligations, they must be held accountable, because a law without accountability is toothless. the brazilian forest, the ituna-itatá, seems far away, but their protection also depends on the products that are on our supermarket shelves. we have the power to change the rules and make products free of deforestation and human rights violations the norm in europe. let's get to it.
land grabbing and deforestation, two crimes against mother nature, perpetrated by a small number of profit-seeking people who don't care about the consequences of their actions for the rest of society. they are cutting down trees, destroying habitats for a variety of animals, and compacting formerly fertile soils so sustainably that only a desolate steppe is left in place of a once magnificent green landscape. by robbing mother nature in this way, man, for the sake of short-term financial success, is taking up space for his own life bit by bit, literally sawing the branch on which he is sitting. the members of this house are turning their attention to the amazon. as in many other debates, this so-called parliament is seriously overestimating its powers, because as long as the amazon is not part of the eu, we should rather look to our member states, where nothing else is happening on a regular basis and where land grabbing and deforestation are being carried out to promote an alleged energy transition that is not a real one.

in writing. today, chancellor angela merkel is in the plenary of the european parliament to present the priorities of the german presidency of the council of the european union. i welcome his majesty's presence among us and look forward to the next six months of this particularly difficult year. the chancellor listed the essential components of the german semester and highlighted solidarity, cohesion, innovation and digital and green development as the main directions of her strategy. the present moment is a particularly difficult one on many levels. germany had already set itself ambitious targets, and these will overlap with the severe challenges facing the entire union at this point, caused by the covid-19 pandemic. i have confidence in chancellor merkel's ability to handle such moments of crisis. the german government's commitment to the european project is well known and will hopefully provide a new impetus to the european union. i regard the current situation as favourable and the auspices appear to be on the union's side. i urge the current presidency to pay greater attention not only to the union's grave internal challenges but also to the complex international situation in which the eu is a key player. we need to continue the traditional transatlantic link and alliance with the us and be vigilant in relation to other international partners.
in writing. the german presidency's agenda for action was presented by angela merkel, with a focus on european unity. given the turbulent times we are living in, it is important to remember that most things work better, both in europe and internationally, when eu countries can agree on big things and do them together. the eu's single china policy is seen as a priority, which is unique from a foreign and security policy perspective, but hardly achievable with just one presidency. germany sees the development of european relations with africa as a second priority and, as the presidency, has the opportunity to make proposals for the further development of the eu's africa policy with a view to reducing poverty and the resulting migratory pressure.
in writing. the european unemployment insurance scheme, described by the german presidency as part of the european pillar of social rights, is a mistake. a community model such as unemployment insurance relieves member states of their responsibility for creating functioning labour markets. instead, it creates a new pot of support, a 'bailout' mechanism for countries that have become financially distressed through self-inflicted debt. the unemployment insurance system saves each member state from correcting its own policy errors and shifts the financial burden onto other member states. in addition, a 'just europe' for the german presidency means that it will actively support the further development of the commission's proposal to develop and introduce an eu framework for national minimum wages. i don't see it that way. this initiative is not only illegal but also economically harmful. it is unlawful because it is contrary to the treaties and the constant case law of the court of justice and cannot be based on the charter of fundamental rights. it is economically and politically harmful because it ignores the fundamental fact that the labour markets of the member states are very different. the respective roles of the state and the social partners vary, as does the mix of legislation and collective bargaining to regulate wages, working time and employment protection. this proposal has a significant impact.
in writing. i stand by the motto of the presidency of the council: together. europe is strong again. the eu is capable of great things if it stands together. i hope that solidarity will prevail between the member states. the challenges facing the eu today are the greatest test of its existence. and only together will we survive.
i have to say, madam president, it is time for the heads of state and government to leave their trenches. we greens will be measuring the outcome of the summit next week by how future-proof it is. we don't care how the 1.8 trillion euros are spent. the entire eu budget must be compatible with the paris agreement. fossil fuels must not be promoted any more. heads of government who violate the rule of law and democracy should no longer receive eu funding. the money is better kept by local mayors and ngos. mr president, mrs chancellor, do not make lazy compromises at the summit at the expense of the climate and our democracy. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i hope that the council will take them up. climate change, nature conservation, the erasmus programmes, but also funds for democracy are permanent tasks and cannot be limited to three or four years in a fund. be brave and show responsibility!
on behalf of the id group. the commission is not in the fight against money laundering, you call for transnational cooperation and sanctions. the right way, for surely not everyone here is blind to one particular money laundering industry: ngos. eurojust, europol, the european court of auditors, moneyval and national bodies have for years denounced ngos as money laundering cover-ups. but the eu puts ngos above the law. with money laundered and expertise bought, shady entities like the open society foundation, with over 20 billion euros, are influencing public opinion. hungary has tried to force such opinion-makers to be transparent and has been condemned by the ecj. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets.
mr president, the european union is clearly a relevant level of cooperation in the field of public health. but the implementation of a public health strategy more than six months after the outbreak is proof that europe has failed in its missions, mainly two of them: first, anticipation, because the european union has set up a specific monitoring and alert agency, the cdc, and it is regrettable that this agency did not react until 22 january, a month after the official information began. the second mission is to provide financial and logistical support to member states in the face of financial, social and health consequences in the event of a crisis. the commission has indeed deployed a great deal of legislative financial resources, but all in a disorderly manner and in a climate of complete impetuosity. today, to call for more europe through the creation of a europe of health, in total disregard of the prerogatives of the state, is a real attack on national subsidiarity. the eu, through its three institutions, would do well to reflect on the health catastrophe caused by keeping its borders open, especially the schengen area. this determination to maintain the free movement of people, carriers of the virus, by land, sea or air, is responsible for the current slaughter.
mr president, commissioner, this crisis has shown that to build a fairer europe, we must guarantee access to quality care for all. to do this, we need a europe of health that allows us to coordinate better to invest in a europe that serves citizens. and i would like to emphasize two points. first, austerity and the covid-19 crisis have exhausted our public hospitals and healthcare providers. europe must support member states' investments in our public health infrastructure. secondly, health should not be the exclusive domain of the private sector. to do this, europe must invest and create public laboratories and research centres whose results will benefit everyone and provide access to cheaper treatments.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. health policy is not european policy - it has been for all these years. covid-19 has taught us something better. health policy is very much european policy. we have seen the state of health systems in the countries and how different they are. i come from a country with an excellent healthcare system, and i'm very grateful for that. i have three healthy children, and when i look at the way in which preventive health checks are carried out in other european countries and the standards they have, i say: it is high time to act. we all have to learn. i think what we've learned from covid-19 is that this is about post-covid-19 strategies. this means that we now have to learn not only to make pandemic plans that act immediately - because this will not be the last pandemic, we have to be aware of this - but we also have to look at the health systems in individual european countries in such a way that we create the framework conditions that have to be taken for granted, for example, the framework conditions for the screening of infants and young children, in order to cushion or completely avoid and reduce misery and suffering in advance by early detection. we have already entered europe through cancer prevention, and we must tackle these issues and we must create standards here: quality standards that are binding. so let us say framework conditions and, of course, respect for subsidiarity. we have found 21 members of parliament from the health sector who are willing to negotiate on this. i shall try to bring them together in strasbourg in september. i hope that in future this will be the anchor for pan-european health policy here in parliament.

in writing. the european commission forecasts that the eu economy will shrink by 8.3% in 2020. the economic, social and health crisis caused by the coronavirus must therefore be addressed by a common eu response. the eu's budgetary framework for 2021-2027 and the next generation eu recovery facility of eur 750 billion are key elements of this. in order to ensure that the financing of the recovery fund does not lead to increased contributions from member states to the union budget, reform of the eu's own resources is essential. the european parliament had already called for the adoption of several new revenue sources prior to the negotiations, such as the common consolidated corporate tax base, the taxation of digital services, the financial transaction tax, the revenue from the emissions trading scheme, the contribution of plastics and the mechanism to offset the carbon intensity of imported consumer goods. unfortunately, however, there are member state governments, such as the orbán government, which are threatening that if the next mff payments are made subject to stricter conditions than the rule of law requirement already agreed, the hungarian parliament will not approve the own resources decision, which would jeopardise the entire aid package. we cannot allow the tool for monitoring compliance with the criteria deriving from the eu treaties to be blackmailed by populist forces, threatening the proper eu response to the damage caused by covid-19.
the agenda includes the report by mr nikos androulakis, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety, on the proposal for a decision of the european parliament and of the council amending decision no 1313/2013/eu on a union civil protection mechanism (com ((2020) 0220 - c9-0160/2020 - 2020/0097 ((cod)) (a9-0148/2020).
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the union's civil protection mechanism is a practical expression of solidarity. it supports states that need to deal with high-risk emergencies: natural disasters, health crises, even possible biological, chemical or nuclear accidents. until now, his work has been focused on fighting forest fires. after all, resceu was born as an idea after the deadly fires in portugal in 2017. europe then seemed powerless to help one of its own. but also in the 2018 maati tragedy, where over a hundred people lost their lives in a matter of minutes. even the most sceptical at the time understood that we need more european intervention, both at the level of prevention and at the level of response. in 2020, we experienced a giant historical upheaval, one that has shaped entire generations and changed mindsets and behaviors: the covid-19 pandemic. who would have thought that after a century of rapid scientific and technological development, history would repeat itself not as farce but as tragedy? hundreds of thousands of people around the world, including over 140,000 of our fellow citizens, lost their lives. the economic and social costs are still uncertain. we must therefore act proactively, even for the unforeseen. the changes we voted for a year and a half ago strengthened the mechanism, something that became visible during the pandemic. his action saved our credibility when, at the beginning of the crisis, governments were making disappointingly unpleasant decisions. we then transported over 75,000 european citizens who were able to return home from every corner of the globe, thanks to the coordination and funding of the mechanism. in march, and as the pandemic was already spreading, resceu set up for the first time pan-european medical stocks, with protective equipment such as masks, essential equipment such as respirators, and with provision to include vaccines or treatments when available. it's been a lot, but i'm sure we can do a lot more together. the pandemic has also revealed weaknesses that we must address immediately. last february, president michel proposed a budget reduction for the mechanism to €1 billion for the period 2021-2027. but events that followed showed how wrong his suggestion was. for 2020 alone, the treaties have forced the commission to add an additional eur 400 million to the mechanism. italy was also the first to activate the mechanism, requesting assistance at the beginning of march. however, the material was delivered to her at the end of april, when the pandemic was already gradually receding. in 2017, after the fires in portugal, president juncker, in announcing the creation of resceu, was calling for a europe that can effectively protect human life, and not just send condolences to the families of the victims. unfortunately, because of the council's obsession at the time, the effort was left unfinished. the report we are discussing today is intended to complete this task. we are making a real contribution to prevention by creating pan-european resilience targets for cross-border risks and linking them to national response plans. we are increasing funding to 100% for all the instruments included in resceu, including firefighting aircraft. we are giving the european commission the right to buy the means itself, when it is necessary, so that aid arrives on time, so that what happened in italy, namely the two-month delay, does not happen again. and finally, we are tripling the budget of the mechanism for the next period, with an additional €2 billion from the european recovery fund, bringing the total to over €3 billion. we want the mechanism to be able to respond immediately and effectively not only to a possible second wave of pandemics or wildfires, but also to any other gray or black swan that comes along, threatening to change our lives in dramatic and unexpected ways.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, on behalf of my friend and colleague janez lenarčič, who can’t make it tonight, i am really pleased to discuss with you the report prepared by mr androulakis on the commission’s proposal to amend the union civil protection mechanism. as europe continues to struggle with the covid—19 pandemic, we must recognise this as one of the worst health crises the continent has experienced in over 100 years. the statistics are harsh. within the european union alone, almost 4 million confirmed cases and over 210 000 deaths have been recorded to date. at the beginning of the crisis, unfortunately the response to requests for assistance was often too slow, or failed to materialise. the honourable member of parliament, mr androulakis, has already referred to this, and once again i have to say that the eu member states were not in a position to provide assistance through the union civil protection mechanism from their own resources. 25 out of 52 requests to the mechanism remained unanswered and 27 were answered only partially. the current system of civil protection relies on solidarity among member states. this means that it depends largely on voluntary offers from member states. this works very well in most types of emergencies. an example is the recent explosion in lebanon, where more than a dozen member states offered assistance through the union mechanism. but the limits of the current system were quickly reached during the early stages of the covid—19 crisis, when virtually all eu member states were affected at the same time. the european council recognised these limitations in its joint statement of 26 march. it gave the commission a clear task to propose a more ambitious crisis management system within the european union. the voice of the european parliament to strengthen the union’s mechanism was equally strong, and the commission has reacted. the revised proposal adopted in early june aims to strengthen european response capacities by expanding the scope of rescue, drawing lessons from the covid—19 pandemic. it would make our system more flexible, faster and more comprehensive, and ultimately more effective in the case of large—scale complex emergencies. the commission welcomes the report by the committee on the environment, which recognises the impact of strengthening the mechanism’s response capability through direct procurement of specific items by the european commission, so that we can better support the member states in case of need. second, strengthening the european response coordination centre, serving as a hub for the national civil protection centres to help strengthen our coordination, and thirdly putting a strong focus on prevention and preparedness, including through the establishment of union—wide resilience goals. these are key aspects of our proposal. we also have the opportunity now to put in place an ambitious budget to support this enhanced eu civil protection mechanism. as you know, the commission has proposed to allocate eur 3.1 billion for civil protection over the next seven years. the covid—19 pandemic has shown that nature rolls the dice. this is our wake—up call. the next large scale emergency will strike again. it is only a matter of time. the way we choose to act now will determine how prepared we will be in the future. i hope that the report prepared by mr androulakis will receive your wide support.
the committee on budgets has adopted an opinion. the commission has already done so. you are right, that is indeed an ambitious budgetary objective you have set yourself. you have our support. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he has not already made a statement on the subject of the budget. that is a good proposal, and we support it. it is also good that we finally have a programme here that has real european added value and not just distribution to the member states. two points are still important to me personally: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area, including the creation of a european network of national experts, the development of a european network of national experts and the development of a european network of national experts. it'll also help with visibility. and that is my second point: because it must never happen again that foreign powers are visible in a crisis and the european union is not. this requires an ambitious approach, also from a budgetary point of view. you have our support.
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i am very pleased to present to you today my report on emissions from shipping. the only sector in which the polluter principle does not apply is maritime transport. there is no pricing of greenhouse gases or emission reduction targets. shipping is not explicitly mentioned in either the kyoto protocol or the paris agreement, although emissions from the sector are expected to increase by 50% between 2018 and 2050. and already today, maritime transport to and from ports in the european union emits 138 megatons of co2 annually. this is 3.7% of the eu's climate-related emissions. that's as much as is being dumped in all of belgium. we know this because the eu adopted the mrv regulation in 2015. from 2018, all ships above 5000 gross registered tonnes, regardless of who owns them, whose flag they fly, or for whom they operate, will be required to report their co2 emissions when using eu ports. these reports are verified, reported, and made publicly available in a transparent manner. the eu has adopted this regulation, and the international maritime organisation, the imo, has finally moved to set up its own system. the commission put forward a proposal last year to bring the reporting systems closer together, but unfortunately it missed the opportunity to move from counting to reducing emissions. and we can do so much statistics, and we're not saving a single gram of co2. this house declared an air conditioning emergency last november. we must live up to our responsibility to the planet and to future generations. the commission's proposal is therefore supplemented by two important amendments: first, maritime transport will be included in the ets as of january 2022. this will, for the first time, provide an incentive to reduce co2 emissions from ships. there is already a comprehensive impact assessment, and it shows that this would reduce emissions from the sector by more than twenty percent. mrs von der leyen and mr timmermans have repeatedly promised to include shipping in the ets as part of the green deal. we are now putting forward a proposal which not only generates own resources for the eu budget - which should make mr hahn very happy - but also creates a fund for research and development of climate-friendly ships and for the financing of marine protected areas. secondly, we want to increase the average efficiency of ships by 40% by 2030, compared to 2018/2019 - for which we have very good data - and that's a very good indicator. this sector can thus contribute to increasing the climate targets for 2030, which we are negotiating in the climate act. if transport volumes remain the same, this measure alone would close 10 percent of the gap we have if we increase climate targets by 2030. we also want to do a few things, such as extending the monitoring of greenhouse gas emissions to include the extremely climate-damaging methane, which is increasingly used in lng ships, and where there are already studies showing that methane emissions have increased massively from maritime transport. we call on the commission to take action to ensure that we have zero emissions in ports. it is about protecting the health of our citizens, who can no longer be expected to be exposed to pollutants. the european parliament has been asked to take a decision on the matter, and it has been asked to do so. i think we have to take responsibility and reduce emissions in all areas as quickly as possible. i would like to thank my colleagues once again for their good cooperation in this report.
the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your report. i am grateful to the commission for the information it has given me. i would like to say a few words again because it may not have reached everyone. as the mrv regulation already applies to all ships entering eu ports, regardless of the flag they fly, who they belong to, or for whom they sail, our proposals to extend this regulation now are, of course, aimed at covering the entire scope of the mrv. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's fishing fleet is not disadvantaged. if you do not want to enter the eu because you do not want to sell goods here, you are of course free to do so. but i don't think that's going to happen any time soon. i would like to clarify one thing because it is always confused. in the efficiency target, we talk about the fact that by 2030, the fleet's emissions have to be 40% below the fleet average the fleet average today. that means the first movers, who have already done something, whose ships are already much more efficient, they also have a much smaller path to go. that is, their efforts are automatically rewarded because, of course, they have already made their investment, while everyone else has yet to do so. this gives them a competitive advantage, because they use much less fuel. as regards the openness of technology, i would also like to say this briefly: if you don't want to invest, you can just slow down. if you reduce your speed by 20%, you can save 40% of fuel. so you can achieve this efficiency goal by simply driving slowly. last point: i've been on a lot of panels now. there are many possibilities, whether it be methanol or ammonia, which are just waiting for a signal to be given that the maritime sector is being taken into account, because then such investments will be worthwhile.
rapporteur. – madam president, arms are not like any other export goods, because far too often they are used to suppress opposition or to kill civilians. and that is why it’s so important that once a year we discuss eu arms exports in this parliament, and that’s also why it’s so important that we use this report to improve our way to control where arms end up in this world. and that’s why i decided to use my report to focus on two things: on transparency and on convergence. in our report we all together reached quite a lot. for example, we are calling on the european external action service to actually translate these 500 pages of numbers and columns into an online-searchable database so that public oversight is actually possible. we also ask the european external action service to build up a joint eu-level end-user control, rather than forcing 27 member states to do like a semi-functional system, and we have a number of other suggestions on which we reached broad consensus in our negotiations. and i think we can be very proud, because they help us to better scrutinise and regulate eu arms exports. but as proud as we all can be, this doesn’t solve the main problem, and the main problem is that we have one common position on arms export of the eu, but we have 27 national interpretations, 27 export systems, and an increasing divergence in actual exports of member states. and the more we work together in arms production, the more this will be a problem ˗ the more this will be a problem for democratic control and oversight, because if member states produce arms together, who actually decides over the exports? is it the member state that puts in the most parts? is it the member state that puts in the last screw? can one block all? do you need to vouch your rights for democratic control before you can join? this is not very clear. and the conflict between france and germany last year over arms exports to saudi arabia clearly showed how easily this can create tensions. but this divergence is also a problem for a common eu foreign and security policy. we were also happy about the good results of the berlin conference earlier this year. we even set up an eu mission in the mediterranean to enforce the arms embargo against libya, and this very same mission reported a violation of the arms embargo by turkey and the uae, yet eu member states still supply turkey, the uae and all the other countries breaking this arms embargo with weapons, and some of these weapons end up with sarraj, others end up with haftar, all fuelling the violence. and mr borrell, i really wouldn’t want to be in your shoes trying to build a consistent eu foreign policy on that mess. and then, my dear colleagues from epp, there’s the arms industry: the arms industry that already today complains that all these national regulations make cooperation super difficult, that arms export decisions are not reliable and that they cannot do business that way. and to be frank, i totally get their point. and dear colleagues, all these examples clearly show that if we want to do joint arms production, we need to do joint arms export control as one eu. what i really don’t understand is why the commission, the member states and parts of this parliament are pushing so much for an internal defence market for joint arms production ˗ even putting money into it ˗ and at the same time, the very same people refuse to even discuss an eu-level involvement in democratic oversight and export control and thereby undermine what they want to reach: a european defence union and strategic autonomy of the european union. i want to thank all my shadows for the very fierce and fair debates we had in the negotiations. we’ve reached a lot, but on this point we didn’t reach consensus, and that’s why i decided to bring it to the parliamentary debate today. i’m very much looking forward to the discussion.

vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – madam president, i understand the criticism. i heard some interesting points for sure. let me summarise some of your points. first, criticism on exports from member states to sensitive destinations. yemen, saudi arabia and turkey have been mentioned, but as one of the members that participated in the debate rightly remembered, decisions when issuing export licences are taken at the national level. so there is very little we can do from the european commission. this is something that is taken at the national level, but we can call for more conversions in arms export policies as the member states implement it. and on that, i agree completely. here we are together, because we think that we can behave together in accordance with general rules which reflect our values. so it’s a member state competence, but we need more convergence on the way each member state implements these competences. there are also calls for arms embargoes for specific destinations. i could also agree, but an arms embargo to a specific destination requires unanimity in the council. you should be aware of that, and you know how difficult it is to get unanimity today. but for sure, we can try to agree an arms embargo, but then you have to implement them and experience shows how difficult it is to do so, as we tried to do with the irini operation in libya. first unanimity, and second the willingness to really implement the decision. military weapons have an indispensable role in the preservation of security, freedom and peace, provided that they are used in accordance with international law, human rights law and international humanitarian law. you cannot pretend to have security, and to protect freedom and peace, without having military resources. but you have to use them according to the law, and weapons of war are, by definition, capable of inflicting death and destruction. this ambivalence means that governments that export the weapons must ensure that they are traded and used in a responsible and accountable way, and prevent their diversion to terrorists, criminals and other unauthorised users. this must remain our ambition: to control the use of these weapons in order that they are not being misused. but by definition weapons inflict death and destruction, and our role is to try to be sure that they are used in accordance with the rules of war, and with the rules that protect human rights. accountability for arms export decisions can only take place when authorities are transparent, and the european union does all it can to ensure that utmost transparency is being upheld. this enables national parliaments, civil society and also the european parliament to hold informed debate, and the council and the parliament clearly agree that the strengthening of the european defence technologies and industrial base should be accompanied by closer cooperation and conversions in the control of exports of military technology and equipment. i will continue working in the process of increased convergence, and promoting more transparency in arms exports in the framework of the competences of the commission and of the high representative.
rapporteur. – madam president, we reached a lot with this report and i would be very happy if we could confirm our many forward-looking proposals in the final version on wednesday. but let’s not stop halfway. if we want the eu to play a role in this world in chaos, and if we want to build a true european defence union, we need to join in arms production, but we also need to join in oversight and arms export control. et je comprends bien que ce sujet est une prérogative nationale, oui, but there is one thing that this european project has taught us: no one is going to come, mr borrell, and give this european union and this commission any competences all by themselves, and no one is going to come and offer this parliament any rights for scrutiny, oversight or control. if we do not claim it and fight for it, it’s not going to happen. and that’s why i call on all pro-european parties in this house – and i deliberately include ppe and renew in this one – to vote tomorrow for amendment 19; to vote for amendment 19 that calls on the member states and commission to translate the common position on eu arms export, the common position that was reaffirmed by all 27 member states one year ago, to transfer this common position into eu law. and to do it, to finish what we started, and make sure that the european union can really control where eu arms, arms produced inside the eu, end up in this world in chaos.
deputising for the rapporteur. – madam president, it is the first time in this parliament that i address you on behalf of a colleague, and not on behalf of myself: the rapporteur, sven mikser, who unfortunately couldn’t be here today. so allow me to give you his statement. as we are discussing a report focusing on the implementation of the association agreement between the eu and georgia, i would like to recall that the association agreement is a comprehensive framework that covers a wide range of policy areas from political dialogue, rule of law, human rights and fundamental freedoms, all the way to economic and trade relations. the report points out that enhanced cooperation and eu assistance to georgia are based on the more-for-more principle and are conditional on continued reform processes across all policy areas. georgia has undertaken ambitious reforms and achieved a lot. it deserves to be underscored that georgia’s european aspirations enjoy broad support among almost all the political parties as well as among the georgian people. the report also recalls that the eu-georgia association agreement and ecfta aims to gradually integrate the country with the eu single market and calls on the commission to be more vocal in promoting and advancing this goal. political life in georgia, as we all know, is lively and dynamic. a worrying trend is the increased polarisation of the political scene. this report underscores the need to de-escalate the political discourse and continue reforms in order to guarantee media freedom and independence of the judiciary. recently, georgia has experienced a period of tensions between the government and the political opposition over changes to the electoral system. at the end of june the georgian parliament passed a constitutional amendment that is designed to provide for more proportional representation and, hopefully, for more pluralistic political discourse after the parliamentary elections in 2020. with those elections approaching, the report emphasises the need to uphold and protect media freedom and guarantee equal access to the media for all political parties during this important election campaign. the elections will take place on 31 october, and i believe that if the covid-19 situation allows for it, the european parliament should also dispatch an election observation mission to georgia. the report commends georgia’s internationally-acclaimed response to the covid-19 outbreak, and i would also like to highlight the exemplary cooperation between the eu and georgia in responding to this crisis. and while assessing the progress georgia has made, we cannot forget that one fifth of the territory of the country continues to be occupied. hundreds of thousands of people are unable to return to their homes or face restrictions to their fundamental rights and freedom. and in addition, georgia continues to be a target of russian hybrid activities. the report strongly condemns the ongoing provocations and the borderisation activities that continue along the administrative boundary line. finally, the report received the almost unanimous support of the foreign affairs committee in this house, and the rapporteur, sven mikser, would like to thank all the political parties and the groups who expressed their support to the legitimate aspirations of the georgian people as well as to the sovereignty and territorial integrity of georgia.
vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – madam president, as you know very well, georgia is a very close partner in our neighbourhood and subscribes to an ambitious association agreement with a deep and comprehensive free-trade area underpinned by common values, shared ownership and responsibility. our relations have been maturing from the human rights agenda to connectivity, security, cooperation and academic exchanges under erasmus+. our norms on standards are gradually aligning, making real improvements in the lives of georgian citizens. we are looking forward to continuing to cooperate with georgia, to agree joint priorities in an association agenda for post-2020, strengthening our cooperation, for instance to fight abuses in the visa-free regime, and i am also confident that our eastern partnership in march 2020 will devise a compelling vision for our multilateral relations. our solidarity to support georgia in mitigating the socio-economic consequences of covid-19 is steadfast. i think more than eur 180 million in bilateral funds and eur 150 million in macro-financial assistance remains conditional on progress made. let me have a look at the domestic challenges and opportunities of this country, and let me make reference to three important issues: the electoral context; the justice reform; and the conflicts. this is a crucial month ahead of the october parliamentary elections in georgia. in march, the political parties reached a historic agreement, co-facilitated by the european union delegation. it created a new electoral system more conducive to inclusive gradual and transparent elections. and now we expect effective implementation of these laws and respect for the highest democratic standards. in the justice sector it is important for the georgian parliament to revisit the selection procedure for supreme court judges, which undermines a transparent, genuine, merit-based nomination process, as you mentioned in the report. it is critical to uphold the highest standards of ethics and integrity in the judiciary. finally, let me also reaffirm our unwavering commitment to georgia’s sovereignty and territorial integrity within its internationally recognised borders. i agree with you that the continuing borderisation is completely unacceptable, since we have seen a deterioration of security along the administrative boundary line with the breakaway region of south ossetia, such as in the chorchana-tsnelisi area. i am also very concerned about the continuous closure of crossing points, complicating medical treatment, contacts and access to livelihood. we are constantly reiterating our concerns to russia in bilateral and multilateral fora and we will continue doing so on behalf of the stability of georgia that deserves peaceful borders with all its neighbours. thank you for your attention. i’m looking forward to our discussion.
on behalf of the ppe group. - madam president, i have to ask you to leave. i congratulate my colleague the rapporteur on a balanced report which i hope will receive broad support in the vote. georgia is on the right track, committed to the implementation of the association agreement and the dcfta, and has made efforts towards democracy, fundamental human rights and freedoms and good governance. in order to resolve the internal political tension, a memorandum of understanding was reached on 8 march, which resulted in the amendment of the constitution and the electoral law in accordance with international recommendations, so that the october parliamentary elections will be held on the basis of the new principles. this is a milestone in the normalization of georgia's domestic political environment, which we all look forward to and hope to see in fair and clean elections. georgian society has chosen europe, so we must support it. we must mutually exploit the potential of the association agreement to deepen economic and trade cooperation. a positive approach, the more for more principle, and support for georgia in implementing the necessary reforms are important. it is important to help young people to participate as much as possible in eu mobility programmes and to receive quality learning opportunities. i would like to highlight georgia's effective defense during the covid pandemic, which is exemplary. finally, i would stress that we must stand up for georgia's sovereignty and territorial integrity and declare our support for its euro-atlantic and nato integration aspirations. thank you, sir.
on behalf of the renew group. – madam president, the upcoming parliamentary elections in georgia on 31 october are a possibility for georgia to demonstrate to its people that it is set and ready to be a stable democratic country. the agreement reached by the political parties earlier this year on electoral reforms is the right step and must be fully implemented in order to be able to move forward and to overcome the political polarisation that has long been hampering progress in georgia. the georgian authorities should refrain from pursuing any politically motivated judicial cases and harassing opposition politicians. it is also imperative to guarantee media freedom, editorial independence and to take steps to prevent disinformation campaigns. also, the people must be guaranteed freedom of thought and assembly. there are still many areas that need to be improved, like consolidating the rule of law, the fight against corruption and strengthening human rights and fundamental freedoms. it has been 12 years since the beginning of the recent conflict between russia and georgia. unfortunately the conflict is still there. the ongoing illegal borderisation along the dividing lines damages the situation, prolongs the conflict and destabilises the whole region. in these especially fragile times of the covid-19 pandemic, it is unacceptable that the de—facto south ossetian authorities have repeatedly denied emergency medical evacuations and incoming humanitarian aid. we have the obligation to not forget this conflict, but to continue supporting georgia’s independence, sovereignty and territorial integrity within its internationally recognised borders.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, we would like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent work. since we have seen many challenges in georgia throughout the past year, it was good to see that cooperation among the international friends of georgia worked. with all our and your support, the major political parties struck a deal on a new electoral system for the upcoming elections. these elections are crucial to georgia’s democratic path. georgia also demonstrated significant success in fighting covid-19. in this struggle, the eu supported georgia with assistance worth some eur 100 million. despite being the leader in the region, some problems still have to be overcome. internally, the shadow rule of billionaire ivanishvili threatens democracy. externally russia’s continuous hybrid war threatens the country’s sovereignty through military occupation, creeping annexation and cyberattacks. we need to support georgia’s sovereignty, territorial integrity and its european future. a level playing field should be guaranteed for all political actors, while the judicial system should become more transparent.

in writing. – in her first state of the union speech to the european parliament, ms von der leyen faced the problem of having her declaration of vision and purpose dominated by the coronavirus pandemic. she could hardly ignore it, and sought to find the right balance between firefighting and articulating a long—term mission. to a certain extent, the commission president achieved this. her proposals to bolster the public health component of the eu’s profile were constructive and down to earth. on the other hand, there was little new where the main horizons of eu policy (especially the green deal) are concerned, though affirming them again as she intelligently did makes sense. however, the commitment expressed to make qmv operational in more areas of eu activity actually underlines a basic aspect of the state of the union today that she failed to address: the locus of decision—making within the institutions has shifted again towards the council. both the commission and the parliament make bold proposals, then go into wait—and—see mode while member states palaver about the issues. regarding this, the president did not seem to have anything better than a band—aid solution, when what is needed is an institutional revamp that addresses adequately the political and social realities.
