mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. our forests are places of recreation and recreation, habitats for plants and animals, cultural assets, wood producers and thus an important and indispensable source of income for our forestry workers. but our forests are also the most important resource we have in the fight against climate change. the new eu forest strategy must reflect this multi-functionality. it is to take account of all the potential that our forests offer. this means that ecological, social and economic factors must be balanced - protecting and benefiting. we need a proven sustainable forestry in europe. but that does not mean that we should leave the forest to its fate or chain ourselves to every tree. if you want climate-resilient forests, you have to allow sick trees to be replaced by new, locally adapted trees. we must not forget that behind every tree there are people who have been caring for their forest for generations, preserving it and passing on practical knowledge. all of them, through their work in and for the forest, provide enormous diversity. my appeal to the commission is that the positive functions of sustainable forest management must be communicated more intensively, because the low-carbon forest is fundamental and therefore the miracle weapon of our green future in europe. this must finally be made more visible in political action.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. unfortunately, not everyone understands this: our forests are primarily habitats, carbon sinks, natural air-conditioning systems, which are already under enormous stress from climate change, and they need to be protected. this protection is ill-suited. today the european environment agency has published that even the forests protected under natura 2000 are in bad condition for over 80% of the time, because of the lack of protection plans, the lack of implementation of management plans and the lack of action against violations. instead of continuing to fuel the myth of climate-neutral biomass combustion - in the true sense of the word - as is sometimes done here, we should follow the science. it says: protect old forests, set stringent, verifiable criteria for truly sustainable forest management and fight illegal logging. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area. i would therefore ask you to vote in favour of the alternative proposal from the environment committee for a truly sustainable use of forests.

although, as mr bütikofer has said, it is very weak because the withdrawal of the us is certainly something that jeopardises its existence and the continuity – to keep it working, even at a lower regime, is not contradictory with the importance that we have to give to the situation of human rights in iran. i know that this is something that all parliament is very concerned about and i can assure you that we will continue working in order to put our concerns, our rejection of the executions and detentions that have been mentioned here today before the iranian authorities. we will do difficult it was to reach this agreement, how many years of tough work were required to rebuild the agreement in order to adapt it to the new circumstances, and even enlarging it could be good, if possible. it will depend very much on the political landscape in january next year. in the meantime, about the arms embargo: i want to stress that we europeans will continue to put our arms embargo in place. our arms embargo, the comprehensive arms embargo of the european union – not the jcpoa arms embargo – will continue into 2023. but we cannot say that we fight to keep the jcpoa alive and, at the same time, to impose an arms embargo on the jcpoa itself – what is considered part of it – and ban. on that, we cannot share the position of the us as they didn’t share 13 members of the security council. all i know is we are trying to do our best in order to increase trade with iran. it is very difficult because the american sanctions are very powerful, but the iran deal is not just a nuclear deal. it is a dual task. it is a nuclear deal on the one side and an economic deal on the other, and one thing goes with the other. we cannot pretend that iran fulfils all its nuclear obligations and at the same time not get any kind of reward on the economic side. this is a delicate balance. let’s try to work on two things at the same time: the nuclear agreement and human rights. we will continue fighting to keep the nuclear agreement alive and working and at the same time we will continue working in order to make the iranian authorities aware that respect for human rights is a sine qua non for us to engage with them. and on both points i need the support of the european parliament and a realistic approach to one of the most important problems for our security, because without the jcpoa today iran would be a nuclear power and then we would be in a completely different scenario, in a completely different situation. i think that this achievement for european diplomacy, which took a lot of effort from javier solana until ms mogherini – a lot of people have been working on that – deserves to be preserved, deserves to be continued, deserves to be facilitating a non-nuclear iran, and it also gives us leverage to engage with iran on these other very important issues, such as human rights, which i know the members of this parliament are very attached to, as i am. you can count on the external action service to engage on this issue. the two of them can go in parallel; one things doesn’t prevent the other. thank you very much, members of the parliament, for your support and for this long and moving session. also give us the opportunity to discuss this matter in the context of a wider debate on the capital markets union and to find a politically coordinated response to this serious fraud scandal. the answer to this fraud scandal cannot be simply to point the finger at the supervisory authorities or to give the european agencies more powers, more powers. there are other areas, ladies and gentlemen, which need to be addressed urgently and which need to ensure that a situation like this never happens again. we have to deal with the rules on auditing and the role of auditors in the capital markets. we also need to discuss conflicts of interest between the business lines, consultancy and auditing, and we also need to think about how we regulate the regulatory framework for certain service providers, and we need to ask whether the rules that have been in place so far have been balanced in a responsible and accountable way. this is all the more so as funding the eu economy from a wide range of sources must remain a central policy objective. the european union's markets must remain attractive for investment and financing, and eu companies must have access to a wide range of sources of finance. after one of the worst crises we have ever seen, our companies now, in these difficult times, need good financing conditions to restore their capital and equity at a reasonable cost. this can only be achieved if we regain confidence in european capital markets. it must be in our common interest.
executive vice-president of the commission. – madam president, the commission welcomes today’s debate. it is essential to investigate fully whether eu investors were fully protected in the wirecard case, as provided for by european legislation. we must draw all lessons that are necessary to ensure that cases like wirecard are detected and addressed immediately. we simply cannot allow again that a company that is part of a major stock market index, that is regulated and overseen by national supervisors, and whose auditor is one of the so—called ‘big four’, could collapse in such a way, wiping off eur 22 billion of market capitalisation. every aspect needs to be looked at – from reporting requirements to auditors and supervision. this is why the commission has been following this case very closely. as soon as wirecard announced that it would file for insolvency, we asked the european securities and markets authority to carry out a preliminary investigation on what happened. the commission welcomed the preliminary considerations that esma published on 15 july. we expect further results by the end of this month from the fast-track peer review of enforcement activities that esma has announced, and from the ongoing investigations by the various national authorities. it is too early to draw definitive conclusions about the causes of wirecard’s collapse before those investigations have been completed. having said that, it seems that the principal cause of the collapse is financial reporting fraud, potentially perpetrated over several years. we can and should already start reflecting on how to better protect investors in the eu from such failures in the future. eu rules and the way they are applied aim to eliminate risk of financial reporting fraud as much as possible. they set out three lines of defence to ensure that financial reports issued by listed companies present a true and fair view of a company’s financial position. this is very important to ensure that investors in those companies have all the information they need and are protected. the first line of defence is at the level of the company. eu law requires that eu companies with securities listed on an eu regulated market establish an audit committee. a majority of members of this committee must be independent of the audited entity. the audit committee has a broad range of responsibilities. for example, it must oversee the work of the statutory auditor. it must ensure the effectiveness of the company’s internal quality control and risk management systems and, where applicable, its internal audit. the eu law also requires disclosure of information about the company’s internal control and risk management systems in relation to the financial reporting process. the second line of defence is at the level of the auditors themselves. eu rules require independent external audits. they aim to ensure high quality audits based on professional skills, independence and professional scepticism. auditors must signal any issue that could materially affect the judgement of investors and other users of financial information provided by companies. the auditor of listed companies must also report suspected irregularities, including fraud, to the authorities. the third line of defence is at the level of supervision. national authorities must carry out the daily supervision of financial reporting by companies listed on regulated markets. competent authorities must ensure that financial statements are drawn up in accordance with the relevant reporting framework and that they take appropriate measures when this is not the case. eu legislation on audits also requires national authorities to supervise the activities of statutory auditors. if appropriate, national authorities should also take enforcement actions, including by imposing sanctions. supervision is a crucial element of eu policy, at a time when european financial markets are rapidly evolving. new types of business models and players bring innovation and benefits to citizens and companies, both in terms of cost and greater competition. but they need to be supervised in accordance with the regulatory framework. the wirecard case raises serious questions about the effectiveness of all the three lines of defence. we need to take necessary actions on all fronts, including amending eu law where needed. the commission has already identified certain areas that are worth exploring. on corporate governance, these may include reinforcing a listed company’s management responsibility for the design and proper functioning of internal controls of financial reporting. we could also propose to require compliance with corporate governance requirements like establishing an audit committee as a condition for listing on a regulated market. on external audit, changes could include reinforcing the role of auditors as regards the risks of fraud and the effectiveness of internal controls on financial reporting. on supervision, we could consider strengthening the alertness and response by supervisory authorities to early warning signals. esma could be given more powers towards national competent authorities on enforcement activities. national supervisory activities could be made more transparent, both towards the european authorities, but also possibly towards the public. and we could reflect on reinforcing audit oversight and enforcement at national and european level. again, as i said earlier, we will have more knowledge of what action is needed once the investigations have been completed. but in any case it is clear that we will need to react to this. thank you very much. i look forward to hearing your views on this important issue.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. the wirecard case illustrates one thing above all: the particular challenge of concentrated criminal energy, which is mainly tackled by means of the prosecution rather than by means of the financial supervisory authorities. if we read today that a bank in austria has been playing the regulators for over 30 years with criminal energy, it shows that we do not have a problem of supervision, but a problem of how we can use regulatory rules to detect criminal schemes. the first question, mr president-in-office, is of course what role national supervision has played in the country concerned. after all, you could read in the international press that something might be wrong, and yet bafin has weighed everything. the question of whether a small bank with a huge business area is properly supervised when the small bank is supervised by bafin and the rest by the competent local trade supervisory authority is also to be answered. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. secondly, i am quite open about the need to strengthen the european authorities, even if the federal minister for finance is now calling for it: he was already in office when we negotiated the review of the european supervisors. he was not leading the movement to give the european supervisory authority more powers of enforcement, but he was in the brake house. that is why we should also make a clear statement on this. so i think i'm not one of those people who already knows where the solutions are, because we have to look at the problems more closely. i repeat: wherever criminal energy is used, we will be unable to really master this problem with tick boxes - with forms where you check whether something has been completed. i think that is why it makes sense to think about a special prosecutor's office at european level, as we do with financial interests. that would be the right approach, not tougher rules in places where nothing can be achieved.

on behalf of the id group. the commission is not prepared to accept this. back in 2015, the financial times published a series of articles with the telling name house of wirecard. there was talk of corruption and balance sheet fraud, but auditors, bafin and the german audit office (dpr) certified wirecard's financial conduct as impeccable. the commission also found that the dprk had not provided sufficient evidence to support its claim that the measures were in breach of the undertaking. and that's how wirecard rose to become a dax company. in september 2019, chancellor merkel, during her trip to china, still sat down for wirecard despite warnings from the german embassy in beijing, which was concerned only because of newspaper reports. the company was already under investigation by the singapore authorities. meanwhile, the munich prosecutor's office is also investigating for suspicion of money laundering. it is clear that no authority considered itself competent until the company's insolvency. the bafin classified wirecard as a technology group, while the land of lower bavaria classified wirecard as a financial group. the eu authorities deny any responsibility, as they only dream of the green economy. wirecard was the first dax company ever to file for bankruptcy, with €1.9 billion missing from its balance sheet. two board members have been arrested, one is on the run. everything points to a total failure of the german and eu authorities and to political protection of windy financial investors no different than before the 2008 financial crisis. and it's not just wirecard that is pointing to the next financial disaster.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the period of validity of the directive to the period of 1 january 1999. the council has already taken a decision on this. this parliament has been representing a guiding idea for many, many years. the guiding principle of financial supervision is that large european players are subject to common supervision, while smaller players remain subject to national supervision in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity. what we see here is that in the fintech sector, wirecard in germany has become a large company that has had a pan-european significance, but it has not been properly supervised by the german financial supervisor and it has not been properly supervised by the other authorities. the lesson to be drawn from this is that the principle of national supervision of large players in the internal market must be overcome in the sense of a genuine, functioning capital markets union. what i have unfortunately heard here - the german presidency's warning again against the alleged panacea of additional european powers - is an anachronism after this scandal. because what we've seen is that not only in the press, but also in the board of supervisors of the financial regulators, there's been a warning about the wirecard scandal. the bafin wiped everything away behind closed doors and said: 'we are fine.' so let us draw the consequences here! i hope that you will support a strengthened european financial supervision, which is actually responsible for all the major players, so that we can overcome economic nationalism here.

executive vice-president of the commission. – madam president, today’s debate is very timely and important to make progress on key eu priorities: the recovery, strengthening the resilience of our economy, supporting the transition to a green and digital economy. the commission recently adopted two major initiatives to promote those objectives: a digital finance package and a new action plan on the capital markets union. first, i would like to thank mr kovařík for his report. the commission’s digital finance strategy is fully in line with its principles. but it is not only about principles. commission president ursula von der leyen committed to responding with a legislative act, in full respect of the proportionality, subsidiarity and better law making principles, when parliament adopts a resolution requesting that the commission submit legislative proposals, based on article 225 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union. indeed, with the digital finance strategy, the commission committed to take specific action to follow up on the report’s recommendations. for example, we will propose in 2021 to harmonise rules on customer onboarding, and we will build on the upcoming review of the rules on electronic identification to implement an interoperable cross-border framework for digital identities. time is of the essence in the fast-moving world of digital finance. this is why the commission has already acted in line with the recommendations of the report. the proposal on crypto-assets brings clarity on the rules governing transparency, prudential standards and market integrity. it aims to allow companies to draw on the possibilities offered by these assets, and more generally by new technologies such as blockchain, and to do it in such a way as to mitigate risks for investors and preserve financial stability. the commission moved not only to close the regulatory gap for those crypto-assets that are not covered by the existing rules. in line with your report, we are also proposing amendments to make sure that certain crypto-assets fit into the current regulatory framework. we also proposed a pilot regime for market operators to test innovations in a safe and controlled environment. the report rightly addresses the risk of tax evasion linked to crypto-assets. our july action plan for fair and simple taxation envisages expanding the scope of the directive on administrative cooperation to cover all crypto-assets. we will come with a proposal in 2021. i also share the concerns expressed in the report about the money-laundering risks that may be associated with crypto-assets. crypto-assets are already covered by the fifth anti-money laundering directive. our proposal covers all the crypto-asset service providers listed in the financial action task force standards. it puts considerable requirements on service providers to protect consumers, market integrity and financial stability. additional requirements will follow from future proposals to overhaul the eu anti-money laundering framework in 2021. the commission also agrees on the need for a common approach to resilience of our ict infrastructures. this is why we put forward a legislative proposal on digital operational resilience. it aims to ensure that all financial market participants have put safeguards in place to cope with cyber-attacks and other ict risks. it also introduces an eu oversight framework for ict providers to financial entities, such as cloud computing service providers. so i count on parliament to make swift progress on these proposals. let me now turn to the capital markets union. i would like to thank ms benjumea for her draft report. the commission shares its priorities, and welcomes its ambition. developing our capital markets now, at a time of crisis, matters more than ever. as your report underlines, we need to stimulate financing around europe. we need more financing opportunities to help start-ups and to help larger companies to thrive. we now have 27 national capital markets that are neither fully developed nor fully integrated. this is particularly important in light of brexit. europeans do not get the most out of their savings because they lack the confidence to invest in capital markets, so we need to create more opportunities for europeans to invest safely for their future. we also need to get more funding for the green and digital transitions and, last but not least, to strengthen the international role of the euro. with our action plan, we have 16 initiatives to achieve these objectives. let me just name a few: helping large investors to invest more in eu businesses, including for the long term; making information on eu companies more easily accessible – digitally – for eu and non-eu investors by using a single access point; helping europeans to save safely for their future, for them to buy the best and cheapest financial products and so that their pensions are adequate and sustainable. the commission will propose legislation as of next year to achieve these objectives, and, with your support, we can really make progress towards completing the capital markets union.

the next item is the debate on the commission statement on the impact of the covid-19 pandemic on long-term care facilities (2020/2794 ((rsp)).
on behalf of the id group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. covid-19 is scary. and it's no wonder, because we wake up with covid-19, and we go to sleep with covid-19. i fear that the measures we are taking to combat the pandemic will do far more damage than the pandemic itself. i am not referring to the devastating economic consequences of these measures. no, i mean the measures that have been taken that affect the most vulnerable in society - people in care facilities. in germany, one in two people who died of covid-19 died in a care facility. what action has the government taken? she's isolated these people. she's imposed a ban on all contact with their families. this measure has not saved any lives - quite the contrary. it was incredibly cruel, it was inhumane, and it took lives. it is in such a difficult time that these people naturally need love and affection and closeness to their loved ones. this would have been possible without any problems, of course, if the measures had been observed. we know that today. this can't happen again. this measure was cruel and inhuman. what happened to the hidden heroes of the pandemic, our nurses who were really on the front lines, who sacrificed? what did they get? give it up. what else? applause is nice, appreciation. but was that all? our federal government has provided ridiculous lump sums really ridiculous. but has anything changed in the basic situation of the nursing staff? the situation was already a disaster: a certified nurse in germany earns a gross of 2 500 euros. two and a half thousand euros! she has to work hard for eight hours, physically and mentally. after those eight hours, she can usually sit for another hour or two for documentation. i know that now, not because i read it, but because my wife works there, so i know the problems. we need a policy of humanity.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the health and safety of the elderly is not compromised by the introduction of a new health and safety directive. the eu policy implemented in greece by new democracy and syriza, as well as in health and elderly care, was that of deliberately minimal and degraded, underfunded and under-supported public structures, to open the way to the superprofits of private welfare groups. the result is the mistreatment of the elderly and facilities that are hotbeds of the virus, both for the elderly and for workers. you're only showing interest because the nursing home cases are filling the minimal icu beds of commercialized public health systems with iv patients. the criteria are cost-benefit for you and individual liability with disclaimer for private groups. so today, the urgent need is to strengthen preventive measures, mass repetitive tests, direct permanent recruitment of staff, the privatization of private units and the state's responsibility for them, as well as their inclusion in the state plan with free services.
mr. chairman, please! millions of elderly, terminally ill, disabled, and immunocompromised people are in long-term care facilities. 86% of the staff in these centres are women nurses, healthcare workers often employed for financial reasons in several different facilities or hospitals, which increases the risk of transmission of the virus. the workers of these centers report a lack of adequate human and equipment protection, which leads to high mortality. the extra isolation and lack of contact with loved ones doesn't make treatment any easier. it should also be remembered that in the event of increased restrictions, many carers may not be able to get to work, others may take leave or be laid off because they have no one to care for their families or their children. we have to be aware that without adequate equipment, staff and financial support, without better remuneration of staff, we will not be able to improve the situation in these facilities, because it is difficult to fight the epidemic without the basic protective equipment: masks, gloves or suits. one of my colleagues told me that in germany the average salary for such workers is 2,500 euros. ladies and gentlemen, in eastern european countries, such a worker earns only a few hundred euros - an embarrassingly low figure considering the commitment and the risk involved. commissioner, action and reaction are needed here and now, not in 2021. i know that this is the responsibility of the member states, but in many of these countries, without the support of the commission, it will only get worse.
president, commissioner, whether you lost your job, your health got in trouble, or you lost loved ones, we all have our own story of how coronavirus has affected our lives, and still does. but the ics, homes and nursing homes throughout europe have been particularly hard hit, and in many places they have had to be hermetically sealed. no one came in anymore. my own sister lives in a residential care complex and was not allowed to receive visitors for months just a phone call or a wave from the parking lot, that's all she could do. many families in europe have experienced the same thing, and for months they were not allowed to see and hug their grandparents, their father, their mother, their sister, their brother, and some form of humanity was missing. unfortunately, everything right now points to this so-called second wave of hatred. let europe, please, take the bull by the horns and tackle this crisis together, because it is up to the commission to ensure that there is a coordinated approach so that not all member states are left to fend for themselves. there must also be strict regulations on safety at work. and the commission must ensure that adequate protective equipment is available to everyone in care, in nursing homes, in care homes and in hospitals. because everyone has the right to do their job in a safe way. and everyone also has the right to know that they're surrounded by their loved ones.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. first of all, of course, my thoughts are with the victims of covid-19 and their families. and i have to say, we all owe you an apology. the problems we have had in long-term care homes have been known for decades: lack of staffing, lack of equipment, overcrowding and lack of care - not because of the will of the nurses, who are committed to their work, but because of the overcrowding and bureaucracy and the lack of documentation. that's a fact. i'm a nurse's aide myself. i know what i'm talking about. we, as a family party in germany, back then, long before covid-19, years before, brought in the k21 concept. and there's really only one thing: when i hear "care centers", i hear it all wrong. what we need is 'ambulant versus inpatient' for people to live their lives independently. 'walking before inpatient' is also a principle in germany, but is not practiced. we must live by that principle. we wrote it down years ago in the k21 concept. there would have been a lot fewer deaths. i mean, let's stop this policy of failing to care! and just to conclude, very briefly, i always say to my nursing staff, when they ask me how to treat, "treat your people the way you want to be treated. and if you take that to heart, you're not doing anything wrong. i think we should all take that to heart.

draft opinion of the consultative committee on culture and education. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. the debate on the digital services act has shown me that we have not yet sufficiently internalized what it means that the eu has been a legal subject of the unesco convention on the protection and promotion of cultural diversity and cultural expressions for 15 years. the accidental presence of many colorful videos on the web may be entertaining, but it is not the safeguard of cultural diversity or even media diversity. cultural and media diversity will only be guaranteed if we commit all the main actors to guarantee the accessibility of journalistic content and cultural education content beyond economic interests without any ifs and buts. this requires bold regulation that starts before big service providers are important or even gatekeepers. this requires special regulation, because media are not only economic goods but also cultural goods and should therefore be treated differently from butter or screws. the availability of high-quality, credible information, easily accessible to all, rather than the variety of colourful videos, is the basic prerequisite for enabling meaningful public debate in a digitised world, which is the foundation of a free and democratic society. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives in this area, including the establishment of a european network of information centres. and in a world where big data and algorithmic decision-making are playing an increasingly important role, we're faced with a paradox: for the intermediaries themselves, the processes of opinion formation are becoming more and more transparent through the data that is available to them. but the public is less and less aware of the mechanisms and influences that shape political opinion. you can see that disclosing algorithms alone is not going to be enough. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the new regulation to cover the whole of the eu, including the eu's external borders. i think we have a lot of work to do in this respect.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on legal affairs. – madam president, the digital age we live in today permeates our daily lives and even more so our children’s lives. can we accept that algorithms created by international corporations decide on what we can say and read online, what human rights defenders can say, what dissidents and whistle—blowers can publish? do you know that youtube’s filters suppressed a parliamentary debate on torture because they confused it with violence online? many constitutions ban ex—ante censorship. it is high time we take back control and do the same with censorship machines used by digital platforms. secondly, let’s tackle the predominant business model of surveillance capitalism. major platforms are tracking our every click, everything we read and write online, to analyse our personalities, our likes, our weaknesses, our illnesses. we cannot accept this permanent surveillance of users online, and therefore we need to make sure that we are not subject to pervasive tracking. likewise, the right to anonymity is essential to safeguard our identity online and prevent abuses and data losses and sales. finally, let’s address the gated communities on the internet. let’s make sure consumers can choose which platform to use to ensure competition and to ensure interaction across platforms, to break the dependency and the user lock—in that is predominant on the internet. let’s make sure we take back control of our digital lives. let me add one aspect regarding the ai reports. there is some mention here of biometrics surveillance that has nothing to do with the report on ai and ipr protection. let’s make sure that we do not confuse artificial intelligence with surveillance. this language on biometrics surveillance needs to be deleted.
adopted on 13december.the commission has decided to extend the deadline for the submission of comments to the council's opinion on the proposal for a council regulation amending regulation (ec) no 1612/1999 on the application of the principle of equal treatment for men andwomen in matters of social security. the commission is not prepared to accept this. artificial intelligence is a part of our daily lives. for example, when our music streaming app suggests new songs to us based on our listening habits. other areas may be less familiar to most of us, but have the potential to transform products, industries and indeed our entire lives - in medicine, mobility or agriculture. the eu has now set itself the objective of creating a uniform set of rules. this is pioneering work, and it is also easy to say that it offers a real economic and social opportunity and can become a great local advantage. there are three points to which we must pay particular attention: first: we need to foster innovation, not hold it back. we should remain open to technology in any form of regulation and rely on unbureaucratic rules. second, civil rights are non-negotiable. the protection of fundamental rights is at the top of my agenda. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union is able to comply with the obligations of the treaty. thirdly, harmonization in the internal market. the only way to increase the opportunities for artificial intelligence for people and the economy is to have uniform rules for the whole of the internal market. this is because it creates the greatest possible legal certainty for businesses and consumers. but we don't have to reinvent the wheel. in many areas we already have regulations, for example on liability, on intellectual property. but we have to look carefully: the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules and to monitor the implementation of the new rules and regulations, and will continue to monitor the implementation of the new rules and regulations. the european union can then become the world leader in innovation.
the commission is therefore proposing to extend the period of validity of the directive to the period of 1 january 1999. the commission is not prepared to accept this. the german government has just introduced a chatbot that should provide us all with guidance in these difficult covid times - an example of artificial intelligence in miniature. i just tested the bot and one thing i noticed is that normal human-like communication is not possible outside of the keyword "bingo". a chatbot of the federal government is not a reflection of the breathtaking developments we are facing, of course, but it clearly shows one thing: there can be no ai without humans as creators, but also without humans as end recipients. our strategies for artificial intelligence must be human-centric. and with these reports, we are taking the first proper step in that direction. we're setting human responsibility for ai. but we also have a responsibility to people. the intelligence may be artificial, but it's only with real, non-artificial fundamental rights that we can move forward. and that is why we expressly welcome the fact that the report on ethics is not just about philosophizing about abstract ethical principles, but that it is a basis for binding european regulations and for fundamental rights. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to ensure that the european union is able to take full advantage of the opportunities offered by the new technologies. if the human doesn't understand the federal government chatbot, then the technology has failed. if humans are discriminated against by algorithms, then the technology has failed. if artificial intelligence changes human habitat beyond recognition and destroys our climate, then the technology has failed. and this is not an appeal to pessimism, but to the optimism of progress. this is the most important thing for us. finally, ai cannot be thought of without a green balance sheet. of course, we need ai for our green transition, but we can't imagine it without looking at the damage that ai is doing. in frankfurt alone, 20% of energy costs are generated by data centres. the internet consumes more co2 costs in germany than air travel. we have to change that, we have to put that into the green accounting of ai. that's the only way we're going to get ahead. no ai technology without fundamental rights and environmental protection.
mr president, madam vice-president, i would like to thank you for your reply. we're debating the digital services act today, and we want to use it to set new rules for digitization. we want to protect freedom on the internet, take advantage of opportunities, innovate, but also address negative effects such as hate speech and fake news. i think very concretely, if we look at my report, the libe report, which i was able to contribute to - in writing. the eu says the newly created financial instruments such as sure and the reconstruction fund are necessary to mitigate the impact of the covid-19 crisis on national societies and economies in the eu. this is wrong. as the social and economic consequences vary from one member state to another, member states should and must also pursue their own specific policies to address these challenges. in any case, the reconstruction fund is a complete etiquette scam. the 750 billion euros are said to be used to reduce the economic and social consequences of the crisis. in reality, however, they are used to advance the 'green deal' and to complete a debt and transfer union. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the message is to spend more and more money, and that is why new funds are created by a new budget. the chancellor has always stressed that there would never be a community debt. now there is indeed a common debt and there is no going back. the eu's debt-taking, despite the fact that it has no sovereignty and no budgetary powers of its own, and the distribution of the money bypassing the parliaments and its budgetary law, is a violation of democracy and the law. the result is that in this transfer union, 40% of the eu population co-finances the other 60%.
the rapporteur. the commission is not prepared to accept this. i am pleased to be here with you today from berlin to discuss this important subject which affects us all every day. whether it's in our morning coffee, our chocolate, our barbecued meat, even in car seats, in many of our everyday consumer goods and uses, there is the destruction of our forests. and we consumers often contribute to this unconsciously. forests are being cleared, burned, and cleared to make way for agriculture. it is being replaced by soybean, coffee, cocoa, palm oil and other crops. the same applies to cattle, whose meat and leather are also traded on the european market and contribute to 10% of global deforestation. because there are no uniform rules or reliable labels, we can't be sure that the coffee beans, the soybean meal, the beef leather, didn't come from areas like the amazon that were deliberately converted into agricultural land. so it happens that even for the steaks we put on the grill here in europe, on another continent the rainforest is literally burning. europe's largest meat processors and steakhouse chains regularly source their products from leading brazilian meat producers, who are associated with the destruction of forests, despite their own commitment not to cut down forests. even european cars are deforested: recently, ngos have revealed that a well-known german car brand buys the leather for some of its luxury cars from italian suppliers, who in turn source their material from paraguayan farms, which are unscrupulously expanding their agricultural activities into the forests of the gran chaco. in 2019 alone, colleagues, an area the size of a football field was destroyed every two minutes in paraguay. and this is just one of many examples. in 2019, there was a loss of primary rainforests the size of belgium. countries such as brazil, the republic of congo and indonesia are among the most affected. europe's consumption contributes to this global deforestation. and no one in europe should be able to decide for or against a deforestation-free product right in front of the supermarket shelf, but there are currently no european rules that prohibit this. the european commission announced last year in its communication on the protection of international forests that it would take action to combat this. and the work plan presented - and we are very pleased about it, mr sinkevičius - confirms this. but we have not been idle in the european parliament either, and we have prepared ourselves. we are voting this week on a report that sets out the demands for this legislation, and it embodies a simple principle: if a company wants to bring products to the eu market - the largest in the world - it must demonstrate that the entire supply chain, including suppliers, business partners and retailers, does not include products or services linked to deforestation or the destruction of other valuable ecosystems. but the destruction of forests, the green lungs of our planet, home to 80 percent of rural biodiversity and one of our strongest allies in the fight against climate change, is not only an ecological disaster, it is also a social one, because the destruction of forests is often the cause and consequence of human rights violations. therefore, companies must also be able to demonstrate that their products are not associated with labour rights violations, land grabbing and violence against those who live by, with and in forests. where human rights violations or environmental damage are part of a supply chain, despite the influence of a company, european companies should be able to be held liable for these damages under civil law and to pay compensation. contrary to what is often claimed, companies are not held responsible for events over which they have no influence. they are merely expected to meet their own due diligence obligations. a law without a liability clause would be little more than a toothless declaration of intent. these rules should also apply to financial institutions. people need to be sure that their assets are not being used to finance activities that are destructive to the environment and violate human rights. the eu's role in the world economy is to provide a framework for the development of the world economy. this is how we make a difference and organize a green deal that is not only valid within europe, but also meets our global responsibilities. many companies share the view that responsibly produced goods are not a nice tohave for marketing reasons, but a necessity for the survival of our planet. the legal framework outlined here would support companies that are already doing business responsibly. at the same time, it would create a level playing field for those who have so far profited at the expense of nature and people. the european parliament has therefore this week the opportunity to put pressure on the commission and to submit an ambitious proposal. ursula von der leyen said in her candidacy speech that she would take any legislation from parliament seriously if we supported it. parliament should call on them today and remind them of that promise. and we should today include an ambitious proposal in the work of mr sinkevičius so that we do not have a bitter aftertaste when we enjoy chocolate or coffee and that we take a global leadership role in europe against deforestation, for nature conservation and the protection of human rights. and that is what we have the opportunity to do today. please support the motion.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission is not the speed and extent at which we are losing forest areas around the world is staggering and highly dangerous. we in the eu are contributing to this destruction by consuming soy, palm oil and meat. even though we are only 10% of the population, we are clear: we must act, we must reverse this trend, we must protect forest areas! the present draft of a new eu legal framework, which we agreed together in the green deal resolution, is a step in the right direction. we want to make it possible to trace the entire supply chain of products that are particularly harmful to forests, in particular the place where the product comes from. we want to make sure it doesn't come from an area that's been cleared for forest. if we can get such a framework for all companies that want to bring their products to the european market in the future, then we will have made a real step forward in our responsibility as europeans to protect forests worldwide. but, ladies and gentlemen, for this, we need the corporations as partners, because the state cannot monitor every tree and every farmer. we need to take stronger action when the risk is greater. we need measures that are feasible and that can be implemented by small and mediumsized enterprises. we need an impact assessment in advance, we need legal certainty through clear guidelines. i think we are wise to draw on existing certification schemes, improve and standardise them where necessary, and ideally to label them 'eu-audited'. we've made a lot of progress on the environment committee. it is not just the forest that must be protected, but also human rights, especially the rights of indigenous peoples. that is right, because we must also face up to this responsibility. but we should not be back door fact-finding and circumventing the committees in charge in formulating the due diligence obligations that this report is supposed to do. ladies and gentlemen, this new legal framework is accompanied by the great hope and expectation that we will significantly reduce deforestation due to consumption in the eu and thus set the standard for the other 90% of consumers worldwide. it is therefore very important that we put the measures in place now and that they act quickly.

in writing. the commission is taking a welcome step in the right direction with the eu4health programme, but there are gaps in the areas of health care, so that medical treatment is not necessary because of an unhealthy lifestyle. unfortunately, this is hardly talked about, let alone set as a goal. the causes of disease must be tackled, not the symptoms. i would like to give a few examples from the fields of sports promotion and school education. by recognising the importance of gyms as a system and by enhancing the importance of school sports and avoiding corresponding hours of inactivity, especially in view of the lack of physical activity among pupils, a significant increase in health in their lives can be achieved, not only for the pupils but also for their future. in addition, it can be used to increase education in healthy and balanced nutrition in schools and in the general population. the aim must be to convince citizens of a healthy lifestyle from an early age and to inform them accordingly about the consequences of an unhealthy diet. this will gradually improve the health of the general population and relieve the burden on the health system.
in writing. the budget of the eu health programme (eu4health) has been increased to €5.1 billion by the recent provisional agreement between the european parliament and the european council. with an initial budget of €9.4 billion, this programme was virtually sacrificed by the council, which reduced the budget to just €1.7 billion. the european parliament has not accepted this regression in any way, as the health of european citizens must remain our priority. i continue to support the efforts of the european legislator to ensure a satisfactory budget to support measures to reform and strengthen national health systems. with the largest health budget ever, we will be able to deliver on measures to strengthen prevention, increase health systems' resilience, reduce inequalities in access to health and digitise health. my amendments aimed to improve equal access to health care, to modern treatments, medicines and equipment, and to step up prevention actions. i have contributed significantly to better implementation of the programme by calling for annual programming and clear criteria for granting funding.
in writing. for the first time, the eu is laying the foundations for a european health programme. a set of actions to pool available resources at european level to generate solutions for all member states, reduce inequalities and increase the quality of the medical process. as early as 2014, we publicly called for the creation of such a program. we need cooperation between member states, common solutions and more investment to create a health union. we need to raise our ambition. the european commission proposed a substantial allocation, but the council reduced the amount. investing in health must become a priority for building a society in which european citizens have access to the best health facilities. it is essential that these financial resources be invested in improving infrastructure, in training medical staff, in improving the quality of medical care, in research, but also in creating cooperation mechanisms, such as the creation of joint medical units at european level for emergency situations or in situations where member states are facing difficulties. the proposal to create the blue medical corps, built on the positive example given by romanian doctors who went to italy during the pandemic, can show again that together we are stronger.
in writing. i have long been a strong advocate of closer eu health cooperation and of a communicable diseases regulation that would set common criteria at eu level for the control, treatment and prevention of communicable diseases. it is important that we have common criteria at eu level for the monitoring, prevention and control of communicable diseases. the new regulation would be science-based and would strengthen the resources and mandate of the european centre for disease prevention and control (ecdc). the regulation should also set a minimum level of investment in health to ensure that member states' health systems are well prepared for future pandemics. the eu4health programme is a step in the right direction. it is important that the programme enables us to prepare more thoroughly for significant cross-border health threats. member states alone cannot act effectively enough to protect citizens. health is a global issue, and so should be the solutions we seek. we must also not turn a blind eye to future health threats that need to be proactively addressed, such as antibiotic resistance and the global health challenges it poses.
in writing. union action in the field of health is limited by the lack of contracts, but the union should pursue a coherent public health strategy to respond to existing epidemics, taking into account regional and national specificities. the union should help member states to reduce inequalities in healthcare systems and to ensure universal health coverage, to address healthcare-associated infections, to address vulnerable groups such as child, infant and maternal care, ageing populations, chronic diseases and disease prevention, and to promote healthy lifestyles, provide prevention services and prepare health systems for emerging technologies. the experience gained during the covid-19 crisis has demonstrated the need for further decisive action at union level to support cooperation and coordination of action between member states, in particular neighbouring regions, as well as between public authorities and relevant stakeholders. this cooperation should contribute to preparing for and controlling the transboundary spread of serious infectious diseases in humans, to facilitating the development and availability of products for the prevention and treatment of diseases, and to tackling other serious cross-border health threats. it would also help to protect and improve the health and well-being of all union citizens. however, it should be borne in mind that these decisions must be agreed with the member states and only be submitted with their consent. solidarity and unity are principles of the union and the health programme should ensure a coherent union approach to the elimination of cross-border threats to health.

member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, i’m not going to repeat what i have already been saying, that we have been witnessing with great concern increasingly rapid infection rates and loss of life across the eu. every day when we listen to the news and we hear in all our countries the number of people who have been admitted to hospitals to intensive care, the number of people who lose their lives, we understand that we need to do everything possible, and this is what we have been doing as a commission from the very beginning. vaccination is not going to be a magic solution that will allow us to go back to our everyday lives overnight. unfortunately, for covid—19 there is really no silver bullet and it is important that when we communicate to citizens, we are open and frank about this. but vaccinations, once we have a safe and effective vaccine, will play a crucial role. it will play a crucial role in saving lives, in containing the pandemic, in protecting our health systems and, of course, in restoring our economy. it will provide our citizens with a sense of hope and predictability. predictability is something that we often take for granted, but what we have been living with in the last 10 months is the uncertainty of not knowing what the next day will hold. on monday, we saw financial markets reacting with glee to the encouraging announcements from one of the vaccine developers. but i also saw something else, i saw the element of hope that european union citizens felt with this news. we are therefore doing everything we can to ensure that vaccines are produced as quickly and as safely as possible. to maximise our chances of success, we have been working very intensively to have a diverse, common eu portfolio of vaccines against covid—19 as soon as possible and developed using different technologies. the negotiations with companies have been intensive and inclusive, carried out with and for member states. to date, we have signed three contracts, and are about to sign the fourth with pfizer/biontech. our negotiations are also advanced with another two companies and exploratory talks are ongoing with a number of producers. i have often been asked over the last few days, and i want to reassure you, that once a vaccine becomes available, all member states will have equal access to the available doses at the same time, according to the agreements we have concluded. now i fully recognise the importance of transparency in this process. due to the highly competitive nature of this global market, the commission is legally not able to disclose the information contained in the contracts. non—disclosure clauses are a standard feature of these agreements that vaccine developers have also concluded with other countries. this is necessary to protect these sensitive negotiations and business—related information, such as financial information, development and production plans. the companies require that such sensitive business information remains confidential between the signatories of the contract. the commission, therefore, cannot really decide to unilaterally disclose them without the consent of all involved parties. but these are rules that also protect the tendering process and we fully abide by them, not for the reasons linked to contractors’ commercial interests, but also because we do not want to weaken the position of the commission in the negotiations. we need to be extremely careful during these negotiations, so that we do not have companies leading to cherry—picking on the best conditions. so let me conclude by addressing some of the worries that i’m sure i will hear about today and i have heard over the past weeks. safety: safety is a fundamental requirement for any vaccine to be deployed in the eu. the safety of our citizens is a top priority and it is not negotiable for us. the european medicines agency will be doing everything possible once any vaccine submits for authorisation, to ensure that the safety dimension is covered. let me stress this: we all need to have the same messaging because we see a lot of vaccination hesitancy. this has been ongoing before covid—19 with vaccination hesitancy to do with children’s diseases, with measles, and we cannot afford to encourage this. so, what we want to do is have common communication and assure citizens that we will not lower the bar in terms of safety at any cost. secondly, the agreements with the vaccine developers do not change or derogate from the eu legislation and rules on liability. finally, we are acting in full compliance with all the applicable rules regarding financial management and are confident that the process will stand any future review or audit. i look forward to hearing this debate. i’m sure it is going to be a very lively one. i can assure you that over the last five to six months we have been working 24/7 on this and we need to continue to do this because it is possibly the only way we have out of this pandemic, but not at the cost of reducing safety concerns for citizens. i will never stop saying that. thank you for your patience.

the next item is the debate on the report by mr siegfried mureşan and mr paul tang, on behalf of the committee on budgets and the committee on economic and monetary affairs, on the 'investment plan for a sustainable europe: financing the green deal' (2020/2058 (ini)) (a9-0198/2020).
on behalf of the id group. the commission is not prepared to accept this. before i say anything about the funding of the green deal, i would first like to express my gratitude for being able to find refuge here in berlin on a cold november day in the liaison office (eplo). however, with all the sad developments that parliament is virtually de facto almost closed, this is one way to speak to you now. i am sorry to say this, but as i said, the eplo staff can do nothing about it, and they have shown me what is available here, that it is available, and the work they are doing here is good work, and i should like to stress that at this point. i would also like to congratulate the president of the commission on the terms she uses when she talks about the so-called green deal. first of all, the word "deal" always seemed to me to be a negative one. in my earlier work at the public prosecutor's office in mannheim, we locked up the dealers. and now, look, thirty years later, this word is suddenly a wonderful word that you can use and it's still given the adjective "green", with hope and so on. so, congratulations on making these terms into a miracle, almost a miracle bag. when you talk about the green deal today, and now you even say "new green deal", a new deal, you're also reminded of all the things from american history and all of this. really, so it's as good as it gets. what's the point? this is a billion euro you want to finance, and at the beginning we were talking about three billion. these are all air castles you're building here. how's that supposed to work? the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this is something i have to say: this is going too far. and we should not really be talking about financing the green deal, but about the financial impact on the citizens, on the individual. electricity prices will continue to rise, and we already have the highest electricity prices in germany today. the regulation is undermining entire industries and the loss of competitiveness vis-à-vis the other major players in the world is becoming more and more acute. this can't be the future! so we shouldn't be so afraid of climate change. climate change is a fact, not a question, and it's always been there. it was always the warm times that were the heyday of history. but what are you going to do now? i'd rather trust the word that says in genesis that from now on, seed and harvest, cold and heat, summer and winter, day and night, shall not cease. and i would rather trust these promises of god than the utopian promises of today's climate religion.
i have to say, madam president, to stop the climate crisis, we need to change the economic rules of the game. we all know that our current financial and economic system is increasing resource consumption and exacerbating the climate crisis. that is why we must not measure public budgets solely by the level of debt. there are no debts on a dead planet. in future, it must be said that public budgets are only good if they are compatible with the paris agreement. it is therefore right that we will include clear climate targets and a binding biodiversity quota in the eu budget. this can also be a model for national public budgets. we must stop investing in climate change. airports, motorways, large fossil fuel infrastructure projects and climate-damaging agriculture - all need to be put to the test. and what applies to public investment must also apply to private investment. already, major insurance companies and financial institutions are moving away from fossil fuel subsidies. this is good, but we now also need a binding framework to make it easier for companies to act. we have to get off fossil fuel. we cannot afford to continue to extract fossil fuels like coal or gas for future generations. i would like to thank the rapporteurs, mr siegfried mureșan and mr paul tang, for their report. let us now work together to make our financial system the solution to the problem.
i have to say, madam president, the green deal is an ambitious economic programme for a social-ecological change in europe for the climate, for the environment and for people. the investment plan for a sustainable europe, including the just transition mechanism, aims to link sustainable social economies with the paris climate goals. and i am always amazed, also as rapporteur on two pillars of the just transition mechanism in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, to see again and again how much resistance, down to the smallest details, this clever green deal concept is torpedoed or delayed in parliament, in the council and also in the commission. the eu taxonomy regulation provides clear guidelines on what is sustainable, and we have a good, scalable tool. and there is no need to be so fussy about the application, just to give an example. if eu member states align their economies and public spending with the green deal, this is a great opportunity, and this push must come from the eu. the green deal is our big chance in europe.
the debate is closed. the vote on the amendments will take place today and the final vote will be held tomorrow, friday 13 november. written declarations (article 171) the following documents shall be submitted to the court of justice:
in writing. in january, the commission proposed its plan to finance the green deal to achieve a 40% reduction in greenhouse gas emissions by 2030 and climate neutrality by 2050. for something as complex as climate change, it would cost the comfortable round number of 1 trillion euros. that's a thousand billion. how fitting! to this end, half of the european multiannual budget, or over 500 billion, should be used for environmentally friendly investments. or at least to shady marketers who can sell their projects in brussels as green. to finance this madness, the eu needs its own budgetary capacity. which is now prohibited by the eu treaties. parliament proposes six new taxes to be levied by the eu and directly attributed to the eu. of course, a lot has changed since january. according to the commission's forecast, gdp will have decreased by 7,4% in 2020. in the euro area, almost 8%. this is well below the global economic downturn of 4.3%. people are already suffering from the lockdown. the social liabilities of this health crisis, the enormous costs of financing immigration, and the massive money creation that leads to higher asset prices are already burdening europeans. let's not put any more pressure on the citizens.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, passenger rights, product safety and collective redress – the eu consumer protection standards are amongst the highest in the world. and we can be proud of that. yet the old eu consumer strategy is eight years old and an update is highly needed. what is at stake? the climate crisis is more urgent than ever and to empower consumers to make the most sustainable choices is something that we have to urgently do. with a proper right to repair and mandatory labelling we can make the consumers become agents of sustainable change. many people fear that the digital transformation is going on faster than we, the policy-makers, are able to regulate. this cannot be. consumer protection may not lag behind in the online world. i am very happy that the commission took up in its strategy the concept of vulnerable consumers. there are groups that need a closer look, that might need special protection, like for example, heavily indebted fellow citizens. and we have to make sure that our high consumer protection standards are not undermined in trade agreements. take the precautionary principle: it is a fundamental standard in eu policy-making, yet it is not fully anchored in our bilateral trade agreements, like ceta, and this needs to change. commissioner, i am expecting ambitious proposals in those areas and am looking forward to working with you and my colleagues in the committee on the internal market and consumer protection (imco) on ambitious legislation. trust of eu citizens in eu consumer protection is high. let’s not disappoint them and let’s live up to their expectations.

on behalf of the id group. the commission has already made a number of proposals. mr ferber, thank you very much for your report. the financial markets directive is an important piece of legislation aimed at promoting the development of the currently underdeveloped capital markets in the eu, while ensuring investor and consumer protection. especially in times of corona, it is important to ensure that our european companies can recapitalize quickly and safely. the history of this directive is also a prime example of institutional uncertainty. one step forward, two steps back. it was only in 2018 that we tightened financial regulation drastically. we've forced the financial industry to shower their customers with hundreds of pages of paperwork, as well as record confidential phone calls between companies and customers. no one believes that this is in any way protecting the interests of the customers. the only people who benefited from mifid were financial advisers, who collected huge consulting fees by helping the financial sector comply with the rules they had created. but then a little miracle happened. in a rare moment of listening to their conscience, the commission recognized its mistake and proposed a review. unfortunately, it's now being postponed to 2021. but in times of covid, small and medium-sized enterprises are having a very hard time finding investment capital in the market right now - also because of mifid - so this parliament is now trying to de-bureaucratize part of the financial market in a prudent way - and we welcome that. unfortunately, the rapporteur has now withdrawn many of his good proposals in order to please the left, and we regret that. however, we expect the directive to be reviewed next year and further deregulated and de-bureaucratised. we therefore recommend that the directive be approved. but what if the next financial crisis breaks out in the coming years? given the huge increase in public and private debt across the eu, this would not be a surprise. we are not going to increase the regulatory burden again. i'm sure the consulting industry would be thrilled. what our companies and investors really need now is exceptional regulatory certainty. the overturning of the rulebook does not create trust in the eu institutions. on the contrary, the more we regulate at european level, the more we experience the forces of a european union that is still struggling to hold together. and you don't want that. here, as so often, less is more.

the following is the short presentation of the report by mrs marion walsmann, on behalf of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection, on product safety in the internal market (a9-0207/2020) [2019/2190(ini) ]. mr president, since i had to interrupt you without you having made any mistake, i had made the mistake, you might perhaps repeat the opening remarks a little. you have four minutes now. there you go, mrs. walsmann.
the rapporteur. the commission has already made a number of proposals. with four weeks to go until christmas, many of us are thinking about what to give our loved ones: alexa for grandma or a smartwatch for our daughter. whether it is christmas presents or other commercial products, everyone wants to have a good conscience about the products they buy. we must ensure that consumers are protected from dangerous products in our european single market, even if these include new technologies such as artificial intelligence. that is why it is important to create legal clarity. we will do this by identifying and closing the relevant legal loopholes, for example in the general product safety directive, without eliminating sector-specific regulations for ai products. we should stick to the risk-based approach, i.e. the higher the risk of the ai product, the more stringent the conditions for its admission to the internal market. we should also avoid administrative burdens and check the impact of the measures on smes. over the years, the number of dangerous products has increased progressively: 29% of the most frequently reported products were toys, and 50% of all products classified as dangerous last year came from the people's republic of china. this requires market surveillance authorities to cooperate more closely with customs authorities and online marketplaces in a targeted and cross-border manner. they must be better equipped to identify unsafe products, including through the use of innovative technologies. the european database on dangerous products rapex needs to be modernised and made both more user-friendly and more well-known. and recalls have to be made in a way that they actually reach the people who bought the products, which is unfortunately not the case today for the most part. in some member states, especially in times of the coronavirus, consumers are completely dependent on online purchases. online marketplaces could play a significant role in preventing dangerous products from being offered to consumers, in their immediate withdrawal from the market and in informing buyers of those products. the measures proposed in the report aim to increase the safety of all products, regardless of whether they are purchased online or offline, whether or not they are covered by sector-specific regulations, and whether they are purchased from a supplier inside or outside the eu. we have jointly formulated this objective and i would therefore like to thank the shadow rapporteurs and their teams for their excellent cooperation. although the negotiations were not easy and were conducted under difficult conditions due to the restrictions imposed by the corona virus, we nevertheless managed to reach a unanimous agreement on a text in the committee on the internal market. if we are united in the plenary as a team, the commission will get a strong signal from the european parliament that we speak with one voice when it comes to strengthening product safety and thus consumer protection and the european single market. this would be the best christmas present for our european consumers.
