i have to say, madam president, article 5 of the eu treaty provides that the eu may act only within the limits of its contractual competences, in accordance with the principle of limited individual competence. any competence not conferred on the union shall remain with the member states. the objectives referred to in article 2 alone do not constitute a mandate under article 5. and under article 11 (4), the eu cannot implement a citizens' initiative unless it has the power under article 5. in short, the protection of minorities is not a matter for the eu but for the member states. the eu may, where appropriate, encourage their cooperation in the field of culture, in accordance with article 167. and why are we still debating eu measures to protect minorities? because many here believe that the eu has an interest in dissolving national communities and majorities into a sea of minorities. and if you dissolve the sense of community and you have broken down each individual into its nine or twelve identities, like robert musil's "man without characteristics", what do you do with your people who have too much identity consciousness and no connecting characteristics?
the rapporteur. the commission is not it was a great honour to be able to negotiate this dossier for parliament. the response has been tremendous, and the expectations are high. i am therefore very happy and satisfied that we were able to conclude the negotiations under these difficult conditions. so the year of the rail 2021 can start on time. the negotiations were not only complicated by the coronavirus pandemic and our working conditions, as you all know. parliament also had a clear claim - perhaps unlike the council at the beginning - that the year of the rail should be more than just a marketing campaign. yes, it is important that we celebrate and promote rail as an environmentally friendly means of transport with great cultural significance for europe. at the same time, we want to start improvements this year to actually strengthen the railway. because without a shift to rail, we will not be able to achieve the climate targets we have set. around one third of all co2 emissions in the eu come from the transport sector. since the 1990's, they have increased by 20 percent. the main polluters are the combustion engine on the road and the aviation sector. there is no doubt about it: climate-friendly rail must be the backbone of the sustainable transport transformation of the future. my personal motto for these negotiations was therefore: 2021, a european year of railways yes please, but right! rail policy must be given priority again, because under the current framework we simply will not be able to achieve this urgent shift to rail. a negative example is the unfair competition between modes of transport at the expense of rail. the tolling of track is compulsory for each railway line; the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem. there are, however, positive signs of a change in policy. at the beginning of the legislature, in the mission letter to dg move, i missed the word 'rail' altogether. rail now plays a prominent role in the commission's new sustainable and smart mobility strategy and this is important. perhaps corona was a wake-up call here, too, because the railroad has shown how important it is to our security of supply. maybe this pandemic has also taught us all that we are interconnected, that everything is interconnected. and we know that we are in danger of slipping into even more uncertain times with the climate crisis. we need to decarbonize our mobility. what do we want to achieve with this year of rail? the development of a connectivity index. the attractiveness of a link can be deduced primarily by the time taken to travel from a to b. such an index should help to identify connections in the european rail network which would have a disproportionate benefit to the overall network. this data will be valuable for targeted investment in future infrastructure development. we know that despite the billions of dollars that have been invested, the european rail network is still a patchwork of carpets and there is a lack of good cross-border connections. we must do better. we are here to call for the completion of the transeuropean rail corridors. but in the shadow of that we must also think of the little so-called missing links. many people live - and today we have had the linguistic diversity - in internal border regions and have little access to good rail passenger transport. we need to improve that. the commission is also proposing to develop a green rail label, which will allow the railway to identify the goods and their carbon footprint at a glance. we must also make the railway itself more attractive and we do not want to leave the companies out of the loop. it must be easier to book international train tickets and to get information. we need a common booking platform, and that includes good passenger rights. the interest of society in the european year of railways 2021 is huge, and we are therefore pleased that contact points are now being set up in the member states. i urge that they be set up quickly. we must dare to have a pan-european discourse in this year of the railways. how much money has to be invested in railway infrastructure - i'm just talking about digitisation and electrification? how do we deal with externalities? how will we tax kerosene in the future? how can toll systems help us achieve our goals, our climate goals and generate funds for the rail sector? these are all questions i hope to raise in this vibrant year of rail; the commission has already made a number of proposals. how can this money be used as a priority for the development of railways? if it is a debt to future generations, it must of course also have added value for them, if 'train instead of flight' is to work. so now the rail sector is hopeful again, and we hope to have a real rail renaissance this year. the announcement of new night train services also shows us that we are on the right track and that the younger generation has understood a lot. it is, and perhaps seems paradoxical, in these difficult, restrictive times, when we are all called upon to avoid travel and to have as little contact as possible, to celebrate the beginning of the year of the rail. but let's think about it in a way that we can all work together to create a desire for conscious, sustainable and climate-friendly travel. after the pandemic, madam president, it is just and right that the eu is there to help in these times of crisis. the goals for the recovery funds are to help swiftly and unbureaucratically and to overcome the economic crisis, while at the same time fostering european cooperation. in this regard, it was a good idea to use the framework of already existing programmes like the european social fund (esf) and the european regional development fund (erdf), which we know work well in the regions. react-eu follows the logic of swift, easy, productive and european. however the recovery and resilience facility does not. it is shameful to see that the vast majority of the recovery money, eur 670 billion, is being spent with little to no european agenda behind it. i also have to say: that react-eu has no obligation for the usage of interreg is a pity. how the fact that in the first draft of react-eu, interreg wasn’t even mentioned, is shameful and unworthy of a european commission. after images of closed borders and failed european solidarity at the peak of this crisis, after months of isolation and for thousands of families, also months of loss and mourning, we need the european coronavirus aid to be used to bring europeans back together. we need our funds to be spent in a european way, a way that increases european exchange, cooperation and solidarity, and that benefits our citizens. here’s how to do it. instead of transferring eur 670 billion to the member states, use them to top up these tools that foster european cohesion – top up the erdf, the cohesion fund, the just transition fund and the esf, and then use the eur 50 billion that are now planned for react-eu to top up the programmes that support european exchange, european culture and european education: to support interreg, erasmus and creative europe. this is the way we should spend our european money. to enable europeans to study, work, celebrate life, live and laugh together, until once again this crazy pandemic is finally over. react-eu is not bad. it is one of the better programmes of the next generation eu funds, and probably the best that we can get from a council like this, but for me as a member of the next generation of europeans, one good programme is not enough. the overall spending is not climate-just enough, it is not social enough, and it’s definitely not european enough.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. the eastern partnership has been in existence for over ten years. over eur 1.3 billion has been invested to date in stabilising government institutions to bring them closer to eu standards. apart from some minor successes, the results are rather sobering. take the example of ukraine: as early as november 2019, i drew attention here in the plenary to the system still permeated by corruption and soviet structures. ukraine has signed the hague convention on child abduction but has never implemented it. children abducted by a parent to ukraine continue to be held there, with the perpetrators covered by the authorities and court decisions ignored. a meeting with the ukrainian ambassador on this subject, which i had requested and promised, never took place. the eu must finally put conditions on its support. in this case, that means no implementation of the hague convention no subsidies or visa-free travel!

on behalf of the id group. madam president, the high representative, mr borrell, has just spoken. i think the situation in mozambique is best illustrated by the example of andre hanekom, a south african businessman who worked in mozambique and died in police custody after being arrested and imprisoned by the mozambican army. mr. hanekom was accused of supporting the islamist rebels in cabo delgado. these rebels have committed heinous crimes, including beheading villagers who stood in the way of their machinations. there is no doubt that these rebels are real and they pose a serious threat to the state of mozambique and the region beyond. and - mr borrell, you just mentioned it - there is also the terrorist influence from somalia. however, as mr hanekom has tragically learned, parts of the police and army in mozambique are using these insurgents as a pretext to suppress any opinion that deviates from the official line in cabo delgado. these divergent views include opposition to the development of offshore gas fields - one of the largest in africa - and to the forced purchase of land from the local population in direct connection with the development of gas fields. add to this the exploitation of mineral resources for the extraction of precious stones, rampant corruption and the presence of private militias - including the occasional appearance of eric black - and all the ingredients are combined for a humanitarian catastrophe. mr. hanekom didn't want to sell his property. influential groups were trying to force the sale. mr. hanekom had partners who also pursued mining interests in cabo delgado. the islamist uprisings are the result of the exploitation of mozambique's natural resources and the marginalization of the local population. this is the result of corruption in africa, which usually follows these things on foot.
for the greens/efa group madam president, mr vice-president, ladies and gentlemen, the situation in mozambique is alarming. more than 450,000 people have been forced to flee their homes. these internally displaced people suffer from the permanent insecurity, lack of access to basic health care and, of course, the trauma that results from this conflict, often perceived as a targeted measure to depopulate economically viable areas of land. i would like to stress the particularly precarious situation of women, girls and children during this conflict, a situation on which we must absolutely focus. the latest unicef appeal on the situation in mozambique, which was published yesterday, mentions that violations of children's rights continue, with reports of killings, abductions, abuse and ill-treatment of girls and women. cases of gender-based violence are increasing. overcoming and finding solutions at multiple levels, in order to address or mitigate this conflict without interference, should be the norm and correspond to our own objectives.

the president of the council. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. i am grateful for the opportunity to speak for the council on a subject of the utmost importance. i can say this as the representative of a country where dual education - a close partnership between state/public schools on the one hand and business and industry on the other - is the key to the lowest youth unemployment in the european union and also the key to well-paid, dignified, demanding but also fulfilling work. i am grateful to all those who help us to uphold the principle of 'learning from each other, learning together' in order to strengthen vocational training, despite national competence. and what i also notice in a lot of the conversations is that there's still a very strong focus on academic education. i'm not against academic training. but it is important that young people, whether they want to pursue a career immediately after school or an academic career, have the chance to meet with social acceptance, that vocational training is open to all people in particular people with disabilities, but also people who, due to their background or the financial situation of the families from which they come, also have a wide range of opportunities and that there is no discrimination and no exclusion. and i want to emphasize this in the recommendation, that vocational training programmes must be inclusive and accessible to disadvantaged groups. the council has only adopted the important recommendations on vocational training for sustainable competitiveness, social justice and resilience a few weeks ago, and there is also a particular obligation for us as the european union to act, because under the charter of fundamental rights of the european union, everyone has the right to education and to access to vocational training and continuing training. there is still a lot to be done and i also feel that the so-called european skills agenda and this recommendation have really given momentum to this debate in the interests of young people, no matter where they live in the european union and what their background is. we believe that flexible and resilient vocational training systems can offset disruption and turn threats into opportunities. in our declaration we stated that while vocational training enables citizens to adapt to change, it should also enable them to help shape it. as the introduction has just said, the dramatic change brought about by digitalisation is also causing fear. many are concerned and ask: is this training, this qualification, really worth it? or, 'do i have any chance of ever getting through this qualification?' that is why it is important that we make workers, young people, into actors who are not just waiting for something to be put in their place. i am particularly impressed by what we have put forward as a joint declaration. i would like to mention one last point, because it is one of the great success stories of united europe, namely the mobility of young people. through erasmus+, we have created the conditions for hundreds of thousands, if not millions, of young people to have the opportunity to study, work and live in another european country. but it's primarily a program for the academic elite. it is true that, in the last two decades, 1.5 million learners and vet staff have benefited from the erasmus+ mobility programme. but that is not enough for us, we must go further. we must also encourage young people who want to embark on vocational training and qualifications to see that europe is worth getting to know. it is also a good tool against prejudice, stereotypes and resentment. this is why we set ourselves a target in the recommendation on vocational training at eu level of 8% of learners in vocational training benefiting from learning mobility abroad by 2025. i am very pleased that this debate is also helping to bring vocational training opportunities to the fore. i thank all those who are helping to strengthen and extend the role of the european union in this important test. here, close cooperation between regions, member states and the european union is more imperative and necessary than ever.

on behalf of the ppe group mr president, there is no doubt that vocational education and training is a key element of the education system and also of lifelong learning in europe. it is a fundamental piece for inclusion, for the development of active and cohesive societies, it reinforces democratic and also active citizenship. and of course it is essential to achieve the employment, competitiveness and social pillar objectives of the european union. but, indeed, after the pandemic, it faces challenges. one of the challenges is the digital challenge, the ecological challenge and also the demographic challenge. we will have to work in a coordinated way so that no vulnerable sector is left out of these challenges and we can all have access to this technology. the pandemic has also hit everyone hard, but especially the youth, and especially the vulnerable sectors. distance learning has increased school dropout, especially for the most vulnerable group of people, such as people with disabilities. the european union must make youth employment one of its main priorities. i therefore call for a coordinated european strategy to make vocational training accessible, visible, attractive and effective, and to stop it being the ashes of education.
the s&d group is not in favour of this. i am not sure that this is the case. when the winds of change blow, some build hedges, while others build windmills, and the winds are blowing that way in europe right now. digitalisation and the green transition place high demands on our constantly changing labour market. digitalisation is not the only challenge. it is also an opportunity to create a sustainable green future. but to build this green digital future, we need to put much more effort into the smart hands and ensure a european workforce with strong professional skills and high-tech knowledge. that is why we must invest in our vocational training. europe's vocational training must be an attractive option for all, ensuring high quality education for all. we need to ensure a much higher common level of quality across europe and thus fully include students from vocational schools in erasmus plus. we need many more skilled workers, and then we must also have the talented young women involved. and it also has a bonus, because a better working environment is created when both sexes are represented in the workplace. anyone who wants a place to learn must be able to get a place to learn. one that's both paid and well-suited for years in the job market. it is now that we must have the visions of a greener europe. because only with a strong skilled workforce can we build the future of tomorrow. it is now that we must lay the foundations for the society of tomorrow. we therefore welcome the council's proposal. it clearly calls on all member states to take this important step and to invest further in our vocational training. let's get to it. this is important.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, commissioner schmit, colleagues, i would like to tell you about kevin, an electricity student in the paris region, whom i had the good fortune to meet between the two waves of covid. he was returning from a professional mobility in romania and when i asked him what he had gained from it, he replied with a twinkle in his eye: "never give up". kevin came back strong with that spirit of conquest, which we want every young european to have. and what gave him this strength was the trust that was given to him, perhaps for the first time, through erasmus. we want thousands of kevins, millions of kevins, to travel across europe, from our villages, from our towns, from our cities. and yet, as you know, erasmus mobility offers too little to learners on the vocational path. among our proposals, we encourage the development of a genuine status for apprentices in europe. it is clear that we must go further if we want to make europe an educational powerhouse, a true educational space for all our young people. it's an emergency. as with the university, we must embark on a bologna process for the vocational pathway. never has training been placed so high on the european agenda. making the vocational pathway a genuine pathway to excellence and the future must be one of the priorities of our legislature. today i want to send a strong message to all our young people: europe is here for you and you are here for europe, because armed with this spirit of conquest, you will be our pride.
mr president vocational training should be seen as a fundamental tool to promote integration and social inclusion. language learning is an excellent tool to strengthen this, as mobility is significantly hampered by the lack of language skills. at the same time, knowing several languages is a great added value in the labour market. i would like to draw your attention to the link between language learning and inclusion. it is an excellent fact that in my own country, hungary, for the first time in europe, a state-recognized exam in hungarian sign language can be taken from 1 july 2021 according to a currently adopted amendment to the law. in addition, the concept of sign language interpreting has been regulated at the legislative level, thus allowing the birth of a new profession. i assure you that you will also be encouraging this very important step in your own member states. it is particularly important for young people, as vocational training will play a greater role in the youth guarantee in the future, and the youth guarantee is a very good and effective tool to combat unemployment and to facilitate the entry of young people who are socially excluded into the labour market.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. yes, training and continuing training - these are the central issues on how our society can and must develop further. however, training and continuing training are not the only things that erasmus+ needs. erasmus+ is important, no question about it, and it's also right also good. but we also need low-threshold offers low-threshold offers, where we can also take young people who simply do not bring this know-how to erasmus+. otherwise we are depending on millions of young people, and we are creating social hardship in old age because these people no longer fit in with our society. we have already had the problem that there is simply no cross-border acceptance of different training criteria. this needs to be fixed too. we have the problem that continuing training is sometimes required in occupations, but cannot be provided either because it is too expensive or because the intellectual know-how is also lacking. this means that low-threshold jobs are urgently needed, and we must compensate for this by means of continuing training, because work is becoming increasingly complex, increasingly varied and increasingly difficult.

in accordance with rule 132 (2) of the rules of procedure, two motions for resolutions have been tabled. the debate is closed. the vote will take place on thursday 17 december 2020. * see minutes written declarations (article 171) the following documents shall be submitted to the court of justice:
in writing. men and women already have equal rights under the eu charter of fundamental rights and the eu treaties. women have a right to equal pay for equal work and equal access to education and vocational opportunities, which can be challenged under the case law of the ecj. the lack of gender parity in some sectors, such as science and nursing, is due to the professional interests and preferences of the respective sex. we reject a shift from recruitment by ability to recruitment by sex through rigid quota systems. we also reject the promotion and application of a gender ideology with more than two natural genders and more than 60 'social genders'. i shall therefore vote against this item.
in writing. equality must be a cross-cutting issue across all policy areas. we need stronger political priorities for this. we already have a forum in parliament and in the commission which deals with gender equality. i therefore support the call for the portuguese presidency to work towards a council formation to give greater visibility to exchanges on this important issue and to move forward on key gender issues.
in writing. at this meeting, parliament called for a strong social europe. how far is parliament from reality? the increased climate action endorsed by the european council will further undermine our prosperity, on which our social benefits are based. this policy is putting the brakes on energy-intensive industries and accelerating deindustrialization. according to a study presented in september 2020 by the german chamber of industry and commerce, these new requirements will lead to a further tightening of the european emissions trading system. the high co2 prices have a big impact on industrial companies, which are part of this emissions trading system. if only companies within the european union have to bear these costs, there is a very high risk that these companies will relocate their activities. so there is a risk of loss for these companies in germany and europe and greenhouse gas emissions are not reduced but only shifted. the co2 target as a driver, the one-sided focus on electric vehicles with the war on the combustion engine has serious implications for our employment. the imposed switch to electric motors can only be financed with drastic austerity programmes and fewer staff. eu climate policy is responsible for the loss of thousands of jobs in industry. this is an antisocial policy.
the rapporteur. the commission is not prepared to accept this. we are voting today on the amendment of parliament's rules of procedure. this change is necessary because we have no emergency regulations and since the pandemic we have been in a difficult and prolonged emergency situation. it is important that the functioning of the european parliament is always guaranteed, because we are a special parliament. we have colleagues from 27 member states here, and we saw, especially at the beginning of the pandemic, that we were faced with particular challenges with travel restrictions and border closures. but parliaments as places of political decision-making must be functional. we've managed to stay open and actually be able to work in this pandemic, and i think we can be proud of that. the president and the committees have taken the necessary decisions to ensure that parliament, as the heart of european democracy, remains truly open and functional. we have been working on this in the committee on constitutional affairs since the outbreak of the pandemic and have succeeded in drawing up new proposals together to be better prepared for any future emergency situations. we have managed to reach consensus across the political groups on four new rules of procedure which are to be voted on today. these rules are also preparing parliament for the digital working methods we are currently experiencing, for rapid decision-making and better legitimacy of these decisions. i would like to thank the members for their cooperative and constructive cooperation and recommend that the report on the amendment of the rules of procedure be adopted. we are thus guaranteeing the citizens that parliament will remain fully operational in any emergency situation and that we will be able to do our job at any time. and that is why it is important that we adopt these regulations in accordance with the rules of procedure.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, yesterday we approved the eu budget and the corona recovery funds – eur 1.8 trillion of european solidarity. but, we have to be honest: some of this money will be stolen. if we, the european union, are going to spend almost twice as much, we should at least double the budget of our anti-corruption bodies: the european public prosecutor’s office (eppo) and the european anti-fraud office (olaf). i honestly do not understand why, in this situation, you, commissioner hahn, have not approved the eur 10 million in additional funding that the eppo requested to properly function. maybe even more seriously, two thirds of the fraud that is uncovered by olaf is simply ignored by the member states. at this point in the eu version of monopoly, we have to rename the get out of jail free card and call it the ‘member states don’t cooperate with olaf’ card. this has to stop. olaf and the eppo need to be properly funded and staffed. member states urgently need to improve their cooperation with the anti-corruption bodies. sweden, ireland and denmark need to join the european public prosecutor, because they are giving a cheap excuse to poland and hungary not to join. finally, if member states don’t cooperate with olaf, we have to cut their funding under the common provisions regulation, because it clearly means that the management and control systems are not working. it has to be clear: if eu money is stolen, we won’t let people get away with it.

external action service on 21 november. we welcome as a first step, the release of the three eipr staff members on 3 december, while reiterating our call for the release of mr george patrick zaki, an eipr researcher who has remained in detention since february 2020. we have also noted that the challenges mentioned take place against positive developments in social and economic rights, such as the protection of women amid the covid pandemic, as highlighted in the latest undp un women report that shows egypt is the best performer in the region. further, on 25 november the egyptian government approved the implementing regulations of the 2019 ngo law, which is an improvement from previous legislation and gives more predictability to charity organisations. the protection and promotion of human rights, democracy and the rule of law are guiding principles of our external action and policies. the new action plan on human rights and democracy 2020—2024, approved in november, reaffirms the eu’s strong commitment to further advancing universal values for all. these commitments also have a central place in the framework of our political engagement and cooperation with egypt. this was also discussed during high representative borrell’s latest visit to cairo on 3 september of this year, as well as that of the president of the european council, mr michel, in november. the eu special representative for human rights, mr gilmore, is also in regular contact with the relevant egyptian counterparts. the eu has a clear interest in egypt’s long—term stability and security. this can only be achieved if human rights are fully implemented and upheld. this remains the guiding torch in our bilateral dialogue with egypt within the framework of our partnership.
the debate is closed. the vote on the amendments will take place today, while the final vote will be held on friday 18 december 2020. written declarations (article 171) the following documents shall be submitted to the court of justice:
in writing. it is appalling that some in france have been outraged by the visit to paris of egyptian president abdel fattah al-sissi, who was elected following the overthrow of his predecessor mohamed morsi, who was close to the muslim brotherhood. abdel fattah al-sisi also made a major contribution to the improvement of the situation of the egyptian copts. this is evidenced by the inauguration in january 2019 of an impressive nativity cathedral in egypt's new administrative capital, and the sharp increase in the number of coptic candidates in the 2020 parliamentary elections. these strong symbols of ecumenism cannot be ignored at a time when christian communities in the near and middle east, and even in sub-saharan africa, seem to be gradually disappearing from the map. so much so that in a report to the former british foreign secretary, the term "genocide" was no longer taboo. these acts must therefore be taken into account in the assessment of this regime, while the fight against islamism is on everyone's mind.
the next item is the debate on six motions for resolutions on forced labour and the situation of the uyghurs in the xinjiang uyghur autonomous region (2020/2913 (rsp)) *.
a writer. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. the resolution on the situation in xinjiang which we are debating today shows the broad agreement of the democratic groups, and that is a good thing. where human rights are trampled on, we show solidarity together. i think it is a scandal that some radical left-wing members of this house have submitted motions on behalf of the gue group in which they express solidarity with the authoritarian communist party propaganda from beijing instead of with the victims of human rights violations. the gue should think about where it really stands. we want to do more than merely criticise the crimes against humanity committed against the uighurs in xinjiang. we also want action to follow. these demands are directed primarily at the political and economic spheres. to be clear, there must be sanctions against those responsible in xinjiang and against companies like xpcc that exploit forced labor. european companies that cannot ensure that they become collaborators with the oppressive regime, that cannot prevent them from becoming collaborators, should leave xinjiang. products of forced labour in uighur are not allowed to enter the eu market. the eu must insist on the ratification of the international labour organisation (ilo) conventions against forced labour in the investment agreement with china. i cannot imagine this house voting in favour if there are no clear commitments in the investment agreement.
author. mr president, the chinese state has been ruthlessly oppressing uyghurs and other muslim minorities in xinjiang for years, and a number of european companies are complicit. the horrors of the chinese state are almost beyond comprehension. of the 10 million uighurs in xinjiang, more than 1 million are in horrific internment camps. 900 000 children have been taken from their parents and placed in state orphanages, and uyghurs are systematically imprisoned, tortured, monitored and forced to undergo abortions and sterilizations. let's call this what it is. the chinese state is committing a crime against humanity, and this week, there was an additional figure. more than half a million: that's the number of uighurs forced by the chinese state to do forced labor, to pick the cotton that our clothes are made of, including by european companies. what we're talking about here is modern slavery, and any involvement in forced labor is absolutely unacceptable. it is intolerable that european companies are involved in this. this cannot go unchallenged. a new eu sanctions regime has recently been adopted to hit the most serious human rights violators swiftly and hard. now that list is empty. i call on the chinese officials and state-owned enterprises responsible for this forced labor, mass incarceration and repression to be the first to add to that list. china must end this criminal campaign today. let me be very clear: there is no place in our european shops for products tainted by uyghur forced labour. any product that violates human rights in the supply chain should therefore be banned. in europe, uyghurs, hong kongers, tibetans and dissidents are safe. in china, their lives are in danger. we will never condone china's brutal campaign of oppression, forced labour and religious persecution. there's no excuse for this anymore. act now, the uighurs need us.
a writer. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, on the television screens at home! the dam of silence and false truths is breaking today. every day men, women and children are broken by violence with the sole purpose of giving up their own identity, their own lives. what is happening in xinjiang is a systematic nightmare of mass detentions, human rights violations and mass sterilization of uighur women. we know from a report by adrian zenz that over 500,000 people in xinjiang are forced to work in the cotton industry. the production accounts for 20% of the world's cotton production. we in the european parliament are documenting the greatest slavery and human rights violation in the world today. we in the european parliament are closing ranks today. for too long, decision-makers have been silent. i want to personally apologize for that. with today's resolution on the fate of the uyghurs, rhetoric is finally being turned into practice. we have targeted sanctions against those responsible through the eu's magnitsky act. the resolution calls for the protection of uyghurs in the eu and in third countries. we do not want another summit between the european union and china at which human rights are not also discussed. at the end of the day, we call on the united nations to make an independent fact-finding mission to china. but that's not the end of the road. tibet, mongolia and hong kong also have significant problems with the chinese government and are fighting for freedom. we need to find partners like taiwan who will stand up for freedom even under chinese pressure.
the commission has already done so. of course i condemn forced labour and i condemn child labour. there is no doubt that the uyghur minority is subject to discrimination by the chinese government. and from my point of view, it is clear that the chinese authorities are guilty of serious human rights violations. i am not advocating that forced labor or other forms of discrimination against uighurs should be overlooked. greater transparency by the chinese government on the internal threats facing the country, an immediate shift away from forced and child labor and re-education camps, and a differentiation between the mostly peaceful members of the uyghur minority and those few who have joined extremism could help bring more stability to the region.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. in china, the uighurs, the muslim minorities and the tibetans are being dramatically oppressed, and this is well known. hundreds of thousands of uighurs are forced to work in the cotton harvest, and this cotton also ends up on the tables of shops in europe. the eu and national governments need to clearly identify these human rights violations, and companies that produce in china need to review and realign their supply chains - they can't help it. apple, nike, adidas, samsung, vw and bmw - these names must stand for responsibility. we in europe have a problem with huawei designing facial recognition software that recognizes ethnic minorities. we in europe do not want to do business with companies that actively support regimes that commit massive human rights violations. this isn't just a security issue. the eu is now negotiating fair competition and better market access with china in the investment protection agreement. we now expect the german presidency of the council of the european union to spell this out clearly. we do not want products of chinese forced labour in europe. china must ratify the ilo core standards, and human rights are non-negotiable! in no other city in europe do so many uighurs live as in munich, where i come from. dear mehriban, the fate and welfare of the uighurs cannot be indifferent to us in europe!
author. – madam president, nasrin sotoudeh is a human rights lawyer. this means that she uses the laws of her country, iran, to defend her clients, the people of iran, against a regime that does not feel bound by any of these laws. the more nasrin uses these laws, the more absurd become the charges of that regime. the latest one: 33 years and 148 lashes for defending women that took off their hijabs. nasrin has now been 10 years in and out of prison in one of the countries with the cruellest prison conditions, amidst the raging covid pandemic. nasrin sotoudeh really pays a high price for her love of the law and of the people of iran. i’m full of admiration for this unbreakable woman and i think she rightfully deserved the sakharov prize from this parliament. as you all know, nasrin is not the only one. the regime’s repression got a lot crueller recently, with arrests and executions. ruhollah zam was executed last week, while ahmadreza djalali is threatened with execution, and no justice has been done for the thousands of protesters in the streets of iran last year. that is why i think it’s so important that we send two clear signals today. one signal to the regime that if they continue like this they will be even more isolated and subject to targeted sanctions. and one signal to the brave iranians, the vibrant and diverse civil society. you have our full solidarity, our full attention, our full support, as long as this is needed. because, just like nasrin, i hope that one day all the prisons of iran will be turned into museums and parks. (applause)
i have to say, madam president, how many more people have to die? that was my first thought when i heard about the execution of ruhollah zam. my second? the mullahs' regime seems to have no other response to the growing criticism and pressure from the population than brutal violence. the eu is also committed to the development of a comprehensive and integrated approach to the issue of nuclear safety, which will be the basis for the eu's policy on nuclear safety. but i miss clear words especially actions. the eeas did not mention in one syllable that the execution was of a political prisoner whose execution was a violation of freedom of expression, freedom of the press, our european values and human rights. mr borrell, honourable members of the house, we must prevent the execution of any more political prisoners. the next time you have an exchange with the regime, put your finger in the wound, speak out against human rights violations. talk directly to the democratic opposition. let us support role models like nasrin sotudeh or the 14 women who called for chameney's resignation in their direct opposition to the regime. for a peaceful future and a balance of power in the middle east, we need a democratic iran that defends human rights and does not trample on them. i hope that next year we will have good reason to propose the sakharov prize to the iranian opposition and to award it to them.
madam president, the eu commenced trade negotiations with excessive demands for ecj jurisdiction, regulatory alignment on eu terms and maximum eu fishing rights. britain wanted a canada-style deal and regulatory sovereignty. since then, the eu gave way on the ecj in return for uk acceptance of the regulatory status quo. negotiations also shifted from dynamic realignment to rules that would allow brussels to offset high eu costs, imposed by future eu climate change standards, not applicable in the uk. if such sanctions respect wto ceilings and leave britain to trade freely with other countries, uk sovereignty would not be unduly abridged. germany has a huge trade surplus with britain. france gains too. uk trade is vital to ireland, whilst eu trade matters even more to the british economy. in these circumstances, a trade deal is almost an imperative of reason. may i therefore remind the eu that trade deals are about trade and mutual advantage and not about climate change or world rescue missions. nor is it wise to make every issue a point of principle. let me close, unusually perhaps, on a positive note. if reason prevails and a deal is struck, this will be due in no small measure to michel barnier’s commitment, acumen and diplomatic sensitivity. and as for the charge, he’s bypassing parliament, that is the very last thing that crossed my mind as a member of the uk coordination group.
madam president, first of all i want to thank michel barnier and all the negotiators for their endless effort. nevertheless we in a very uncomfortable situation. we have two weeks before the deadline and no one knows what will happen. yes, it is good that we are still negotiating, and it is good that we could prevent a no deal so far, but the situation we are in is really unacceptable. business and citizens do not know what will happen, and the european parliament itself finds in a very difficult dilemma – either, if there is a deal we are creating huge chaos on 1 january, or we just give up our scrutiny rights. it is absolutely clear a deal with a strong level playing field is still the best outcome for everyone, but we at the european parliament cannot make a fool of ourselves and just let go out of hand our scrutiny riots. we are not just a rubber stamp institution and we need proper time. for me the time until 28 december is far too short, and i think we should push for technical prolongation in order to have proper time for scrutiny.
